\begin{center}
\rev{\textbf{Revision key.} Red text is new or materially revised relative to the supplied manuscript. The main paper and the complete supplementary material are retained in this single file. All mathematical comments that previously appeared in the appendix have now been implemented as corrected statements and proofs; no unresolved proof-comment boxes remain. Experimental designs and numerical values are not substantively changed.}
\end{center}

\begin{abstract}
\begin{revision}
We study nonparametric conditional-quantile estimation for observations indexed by time and space. Observations collected at nearby times may be dependent, while observations at the same time share a time-specific random spatial effect. We analyze constrained empirical quantile-risk minimizers over bounded convex and nonconvex function classes. The theory allows heavy-tailed measurement errors and imposes no moment condition on the response noise. The main bound is expressed through a blockwise localized Rademacher fixed point and an additional term measuring the fluctuation induced by the random spatial effect. We specialize the framework to univariate trend filtering, high-dimensional linear quantile prediction, $K$-nearest-neighbor graph fused lasso, and clipped sparse ReLU networks for hierarchical composition models. For trend filtering, the usual minimax exponent is retained up to logarithmic factors and the spatial-effect term. We also provide ADMM algorithms and numerical studies under light- and heavy-tailed noise.
\end{revision}
\end{abstract}

\begin{keywords}
\rev{total variation, nonparametric quantile regression, spatio-temporal dependence, beta mixing, localized Rademacher complexity}
\end{keywords}

% \input{quantile_temporal_spatial_v14}

\section{Introduction}


Technological advancements have significantly improved data collection and analysis techniques, resulting in an abundance of data with both temporal and spatial dimensions across various disciplines (\cite{wen2019novel}, \cite{briz2020spatio}, \cite{liu2020high}, \cite{tian2021assessing}, \cite{jayasiri2022spatio}). This proliferation of complex datasets has necessitated the development and adoption of advanced temporal-spatial models designed to address the unique challenges and harness the full potential of temporally and spatially diverse data.

Current leading results for temporal-spatial estimators typically assume that error distributions are sub-Gaussian. In contrast, quantile regression, known for its robustness and versatility across different sectors (\cite{coad2006innovation, sanderson2006statistically, perlich2007high, benoit2009benefits, wasko2014quantile, pata2015astronomical, belloni2023high,zhang2023risk}), often requires fewer presuppositions about error distributions. This approach diverges from conventional models that rely heavily on such assumptions. We extend the quantile regression approach to incorporate a thorough theory of risk bounds for an extensive array of quantile temporal-spatial functions, doing so with minimal assumptions about error variables. This analytical enhancement broadens the scope of quantile regression for temporal-spatial data analysis, bolstering its theoretical underpinnings and inclusivity towards error distributions, including both normal and heavy-tailed distributions.




\begin{revision}
This paper studies nonparametric quantile regression in a spatio-temporal setting. For time points $i=1,\ldots,n$, let $m_i$ be the number of spatial observations collected at time $i$. We observe
\begin{equation}
 y_{ij}=f^*(x_{ij})+\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij},
 \qquad j=1,\ldots,m_i,                                      \label{ass_1_1}
\end{equation}
where $x_{ij}\in[0,1]^d$, $f^*:[0,1]^d\to\mathbb R$ is deterministic, $\delta_i$ is a time-specific random spatial function, and $e_{ij}$ is a measurement error. For a fixed quantile level $\tau\in(0,1)$, we impose
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\{\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}\le0\mid x_{ij}\}=\tau. \label{eq:quantile_calibration_full}
\end{equation}
Thus $f^*(x)$ is the conditional $\tau$-quantile of $y_{ij}$ given $x_{ij}=x$. The shared function $\delta_i$ induces dependence among observations within time $i$, and the time-indexed clusters may be beta-mixing across $i$.
\end{revision}
\subsection{Notation}
\begin{revision}
For an integer $q\ge1$, write $[q]=\{1,\ldots,q\}$. Let $Q$ denote the common marginal distribution of the design points and define the weighted empirical design distribution
\[
 Q_{n,m}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}\delta_{x_{ij}}.
\]
For a measurable $g:[0,1]^d\to\mathbb R$, let
\[
 \|g\|_{L_p(Q)}=\left(\int_{[0,1]^d}|g(x)|^p\,dQ(x)\right)^{1/p},
 \qquad \|g\|_\infty=\sup_{x\in[0,1]^d}|g(x)|,
\]
with the usual modification for $p=\infty$. We abbreviate $\|g\|_2=\|g\|_{L_2(Q)}$ and define
\[
 \langle f,g\rangle_{n,m}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}f(x_{ij})g(x_{ij}),
 \qquad \|g\|_{n,m}^2=\langle g,g\rangle_{n,m}.
\]
Let $N=\sum_{i=1}^n m_i$ and let
\[
 m=\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\right)^{-1}
\]
be the harmonic mean of the group sizes. When a function is evaluated on all observed design points, the same symbol denotes the vector of evaluations in $\mathbb R^N$. For positive sequences $a_n,b_n$, write $a_n\lesssim b_n$ if $a_n\le Cb_n$ for a constant independent of $n$, and $a_n\asymp b_n$ if both inequalities hold. The symbols $O_{\mathbb P}$ and $o_{\mathbb P}$ have their standard meanings.
\end{revision}
\subsection{Summary of Results}
\begin{revision}
Let $\rho_\tau(u)=u\{\tau-\mathbf 1(u<0)\}$ be the check loss. We estimate $f^*$ by
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal F}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{loss_quantile}
\end{equation}
Each time point receives total weight $1/n$, so unequal group sizes are accommodated. The estimation criterion is the check loss, whereas the risk is reported in $L_2(Q)$. A Huber-type loss appears naturally as a lower bound for the excess population quantile risk and bridges these two quantities.

The temporal dependence is modeled through absolute regularity of the joint cluster process $W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i)$, rather than through separate and potentially incompatible assumptions for the design, spatial effect, and measurement errors. For
\[
 L=\left\lceil\frac{2}{C_\beta}\log n\right\rceil,
\]
define the interlaced index sets
\begin{align}
 \mathcal J_{e,\ell}&=\{\ell+2qL:q\ge0\}\cap[n],\nonumber\\
 \mathcal J_{o,\ell}&=\{\ell+(2q+1)L:q\ge0\}\cap[n],
 \qquad \ell\in[L].                                         \label{odd_even_indices}
\end{align}
\label{eq:blocks}
These sets partition $[n]$, and observations in each fixed set are separated by at least $2L$. Berbee coupling is applied once to the joint clusters, preserving the contemporaneous relationship among $X_i$, $\delta_i$, and $E_i$.

For a centered class $\mathcal H$ and $a\in\{e,o\}$, the relevant block complexity retains the weights of the original empirical loss:
\[
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}(\mathcal H)=
 \mathbb E\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\frac1n\sum_{i\in\mathcal J_{a,\ell}}\frac1{m_i}
 \sum_{j=1}^{m_i}\varepsilon_{ij}h(x_{ij}^*)\right|.
\]
The convex theorem defines a critical radius through the sum of these localized block complexities and a remainder
\[
 \Gamma_n\asymp\frac{(s+1)L\log(nT_n)}{n},
\]
which quantifies the random spatial effect under the low-dimensional Lipschitz condition in Assumption~\ref{assump:lip}. If $r_n$ denotes that critical radius and $b_B^2=1\vee2B$, then Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} gives
\[
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2=O_{\mathbb P}(b_B^2r_n^2).
\]
No generic $O\{\operatorname{polylog}(n)/(nm)\}$ rate follows without evaluating the entropy or geometry of the particular function class.

For univariate trend filtering with $J_k(f)=\operatorname{TV}(f^{(k-1)})\le V$, the localized entropy calculation yields
\[
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[b_B^2\left\{V^{2/(2k+1)}(L/N)^{2k/(2k+1)}+\Gamma_n\right\}\right],
\]
up to constants and the logarithmic factors made explicit in the proof. This retains the usual exponent $2k/(2k+1)$. For high-dimensional linear quantile regression, the result controls prediction error through weighted block-column norms and does not claim coefficient recovery without an additional identifiability condition.

For $d>1$, the $K$-nearest-neighbor graph fused-lasso result separates in-sample estimation from off-sample nearest-neighbor approximation. Its stochastic bound is expressed through the explicit incidence-pseudoinverse parameter $\rho(G_K)$ and is proved in Proposition~\ref{app:prop:graph-complexity}. Under the stated graph-geometry conditions and piecewise-Lipschitz regularity, the leading nonparametric term is $N^{-1/d}$ up to logarithms, plus $\Gamma_n$. The graph total variation of a regular target scales as $N^{1-1/d}$, not $N^{-1/d}$.

For nonconvex classes, we select a near-best function $f^\circ\in\mathcal F$, work with the centered star hull, and add the approximation error $\|f^\circ-f^*\|_\infty^2$. The neural-network corollary applies to clipped sparse ReLU networks whose depth, width, and sparsity are chosen to balance approximation and stochastic complexity for hierarchical composition models. The paper also gives corrected ADMM updates for trend filtering and graph fused lasso and reports numerical studies under light- and heavy-tailed noise.
\end{revision}
\section{General Methods}

\begin{revision}
We formulate a general quantile-regression problem over a bounded function class. Throughout the theory,
\begin{equation}
 \|f^*\|_\infty\le B,
 \qquad \sup_{f\in\mathcal F}\|f\|_\infty\le B.             \label{F_2}
\end{equation}
For the convex result, $\mathcal F$ is closed under convex combinations:
\begin{equation}
 \lambda f+(1-\lambda)g\in\mathcal F,
 \qquad f,g\in\mathcal F,\quad \lambda\in[0,1].             \label{F_1}
\end{equation}
The nonconvex result removes this restriction and allows $f^*\notin\mathcal F$.
\end{revision}

\subsection{Assumptions}

\begin{assumption}[Sampling, dependence, and quantile calibration]\label{Assumption 1.}
\begin{revision}
The following conditions hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{ass_1_1__duplicate2} The observations satisfy
$y_{ij}=f^*(x_{ij})+\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}$ and the conditional quantile calibration in \eqref{eq:quantile_calibration_full}.
\item\label{ass_1_1_1} Each $x_{ij}$ has a common density $v$ on $[0,1]^d$, with $0<v_{\min}\le v(x)\le v_{\max}<\infty$.
\item\label{ass_1_2} Let $X_i=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{im_i})$ and $E_i=(e_{i1},\ldots,e_{im_i})$. The spatial effect $\delta_i$ is independent of $X_i$. Conditional on $\delta_i$, the design points $x_{ij}$ are independent with common law $Q$; conditional on $(\delta_i,X_i)$, the measurement errors $e_{ij}$ are independent. Their conditional distributions may depend on $\delta_i$ and on the corresponding design point; within a fixed time cluster, the conditional kernel is the same for all $j$.
\item\label{ass_c_beta} The joint process $W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i)$ is beta-mixing with coefficients
$\beta_W(k)\le C_\beta^{(0)}e^{-C_\beta k}$ for constants $C_\beta^{(0)},C_\beta>0$.
\item\label{ass_1_3} The random spatial function $\delta_i$ may induce arbitrary dependence among the responses within time $i$ through the shared evaluations $\delta_i(x_{ij})$.
\item\label{ass_1_4} No moment condition is imposed on $e_{ij}$ or $\delta_i(x_{ij})$.
\item\label{asm:m} There are constants $0<c_m\le C_m<\infty$ such that $c_mm\le m_i\le C_mm$ for every $i$, where $m$ is the harmonic mean defined above.
\end{enumerate}
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{revision}
The joint mixing condition replaces the contradictory combination of cross-time independence and beta mixing in the supplied version. Coupling the joint cluster is essential: separate couplings of $X_i$, $\delta_i$, and $E_i$ need not preserve their contemporaneous joint distribution.
\end{revision}

\begin{assumption}[Local quantile curvature]\label{assumptionA}
\begin{revision}
There are constants $R>0$ and $0<\underline f\le\overline f<\infty$ such that, for every $i,j$, every $x\in[0,1]^d$, and every $|u|\le R$,
\begin{equation}
 \underline f|u|
 \le\left|F_{ij}\{f^*(x)+u\mid x\}-F_{ij}\{f^*(x)\mid x\}\right|
 \le\overline f|u|,                                          \label{eq:curvature_full}
\end{equation}
where $F_{ij}(\cdot\mid x)$ is the conditional distribution function of $y_{ij}$ given $x_{ij}=x$, and $F_{ij}\{f^*(x)\mid x\}=\tau$.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Low-dimensional Lipschitz spatial fluctuation]\label{assump:lip}
\begin{revision}
Let $U_{ij}=\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}$ and define
\begin{equation}
 D_i(x,u)=\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid x_{i1}=x)
 -\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid\delta_i,x_{i1}=x).               \label{pi_def_assump}
\end{equation}
There is a coordinate set $S\subset[d]$, $|S|=s$, such that $D_i(x,u)$ depends on $x$ only through $x_S$. Almost surely, the map $(x_S,u)\mapsto D_i(x_S,u)$ is uniformly Lipschitz on $[0,1]^s\times[-T_n,T_n]$, with a deterministic constant that does not depend on $i$ or $n$. Since $\mathbb E\{D_i(x,u)\}=0$, the joint-block coupling and a finite-grid argument imply, rather than assume,
\begin{equation}
 \max_I\sup_{x\in[0,1]^d,\ |u|\le T_n}
 \left|\frac1{|I|}\sum_{i\in I}D_i(x,u)\right|
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{(s+1)L\log\{n(1+T_n)\}}{n}}\right\}. \label{lip_for_Pi}
\end{equation}
The complete derivation is given in Lemma~\ref{app:lem:grid-Pi}.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Localization range]\label{ass:tail_multi_d}
\begin{revision}
Set $T_n=2B$. If the envelope $B=B_n$ is allowed to grow, assume only that $\log\{n(1+T_n)\}=O(\log n)$. Because $|f(x)-f^*(x)|\le2B$, every CDF increment in the proof is evaluated on $[-T_n,T_n]$. No tail probability or moment condition on $e_{ij}$ or $\delta_i(x_{ij})$ is required.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{example}[Heavy-tailed errors]\label{exp_tail}
\begin{revision}
The theory permits Cauchy, Student-$t$, and other heavy-tailed measurement errors without any moment restriction. The required conditions concern the conditional quantile calibration, the local CDF slope near the target quantile, and the Lipschitz behavior of the conditional CDF difference in \eqref{pi_def_assump}; they do not require truncating the realized response or controlling $\max_{i,j}|e_{ij}|$.
\end{revision}
\end{example}

\subsection{Results for General Methods}

\begin{revision}
For $a\in\{e,o\}$, $\ell\in[L]$, and a centered class $\mathcal H$, define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}(\mathcal H)=
 \mathbb E\left[\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\frac1n\sum_{i\in\mathcal J_{a,\ell}}\frac1{m_i}
 \sum_{j=1}^{m_i}\varepsilon_{ij}h(x_{ij}^*)\right|\right]. \label{rademacher_def_blocks}
\end{equation}
The denominator and weights are those of the full empirical criterion. If one instead uses a within-block normalization, it must be multiplied by that block's weight in the full objective.

Let $b_B=(1\vee2B)^{1/2}$ and
\[
 \mathcal H_{\mathcal F}(r)=\{f-f^*:f\in\mathcal F,\ \|f-f^*\|_2\le r\}.
\]
Set
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_n=\frac{(s+1)L\log\{n(1+T_n)\}}{n},                  \label{eq:Gamma_n_full}
\end{equation}
and, for a sufficiently large universal constant $C_*$, define
\begin{align}
 \Psi_n(r)&=C_*\left[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^L\sum_{a\in\{e,o\}}
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}\{\mathcal H_{\mathcal F}(c_0^{-1/2}b_Br)\}
 +b_B^2\Gamma_n\right],\nonumber\\
 r_n&=\inf\{r>0:\Psi_n(r)\le r^2/64\}.                      \label{eq:critical_radius}
\end{align}
A nondecreasing sub-root majorant of the complexity term is used when needed.
\end{revision}

\subsection{For $\mathcal F$ as a Convex Set}
\begin{theorem}[Convex spatio-temporal quantile regression]\label{Theorem_1}
\begin{revision}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{Assumption 1.}, \ref{assumptionA}, \ref{assump:lip}, and \ref{ass:tail_multi_d} hold, $\mathcal F$ is convex, $f^*\in\mathcal F$, and \eqref{F_2} holds. Let
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal F}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{main_est}
\end{equation}
If $r_n$ is defined by \eqref{eq:critical_radius}, then
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2=O_{\mathbb P}(b_B^2r_n^2).           \label{eq:general_convex_rate_full}
\end{equation}
More explicitly, for every $\epsilon>0$ there is $C_\epsilon<\infty$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\[
 \mathbb P\{\|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2>C_\epsilon b_B^2r_n^2\}
 \le\epsilon+C n\beta_W(2L).
\]
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}

\begin{revision}
The theorem is stated through a critical radius because the rate depends on the local geometry of $\mathcal F$. The proof uses Knight's identity, local quantile curvature, a single joint-cluster coupling, centered conditional symmetrization, contraction, and the blockwise spatial-effect bound. Since $|f-f^*|\le2B$,
$\|f-f^*\|_2^2\le(1\vee2B)\Delta^2(f-f^*)$; this corrects the factor used in the supplied proof.
\end{revision}


\section{Application To Trend Filtering}

Over the past decade, trend filtering has become a widely used nonparametric method for fitting piecewise-polynomial functions \citep{rudin1992nonlinear,mammen1997locally,tibshirani2005sparsity,kim2009ell_1,tibshirani2014adaptive}.

\begin{definition}[Trend-filtering class]\label{def_tf}
\begin{revision}
For $d=1$, an integer $k\ge1$, and $V>0$, let
\begin{equation}
 J_k(f)=\operatorname{TV}\{f^{(k-1)}\},\qquad
 \mathcal F_k(V)=\{f\in\mathcal F:J_k(f)\le V\}.              \label{def_tvspace_tf}
\end{equation}
For a univariate function $g$,
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(g)=\sup_{0\le t_1<\cdots<t_M\le1}
 \sum_{q=1}^{M-1}|g(t_{q+1})-g(t_q)|.
\]
If $g$ is absolutely continuous, then $\operatorname{TV}(g)=\int_0^1|g'(t)|dt$. The fitted pieces have degree $k-1$: $k=1$ gives a piecewise-constant fit and $k=2$ a piecewise-linear fit.
\end{revision}
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Trend-filtering target]\label{ass_tf}
\begin{revision}
The target satisfies $f^*\in\mathcal F_k(V)$ for a fixed integer $k\ge1$ and a radius $V\ge J_k(f^*)$. In the canonical regime, $V=O(1)$ and $\|f^*\|_\infty\le B$. No mean-zero constraint is required solely for identifiability because the loss identifies the intercept; a centering constraint may be added for a particular parameterization.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\subsection{Result for Quantile Temporal-Spatial Trend Filtering}
\begin{revision}
The constrained estimator is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal F_k(V)}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{eq172}
\end{equation}
\end{revision}

\begin{theorem}[Trend filtering]\label{Theorem_2}
\begin{revision}
Suppose the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} and Assumption~\ref{ass_tf} hold. Assume the empirical entropy of the localized trend-filtering class satisfies the standard bound
\[
 \log N\{\epsilon,\mathcal F_k(V),\|\cdot\|_{n,m}\}
 \lesssim V^{1/k}\epsilon^{-1/k}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[
 b_B^2\left\{V^{2/(2k+1)}\left(\frac{L}{N}\right)^{2k/(2k+1)}
 +\Gamma_n\right\}\right].                                  \label{tf_rate}
\end{equation}
For bounded $V$ and $B$, the leading exponent is $2k/(2k+1)$, up to the logarithmic factor from $L\asymp\log n$ and the spatial-effect term $\Gamma_n$.
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}

\begin{revision}
The block calculation must retain each block's weight in the full empirical criterion. If $M_I\asymp N/L$ is the number of observations in a block, a normalized within-block Dudley bound is multiplied by $M_I/N$. Summing over the $2L$ blocks gives a stochastic term of order $V^{1/(2k)}r^{1-1/(2k)}\sqrt{L/N}$ and hence the fixed point in \eqref{tf_rate}.
\end{revision}


\section{Application To High-dimensional Quantile Linear Regression}

We next consider the constrained $\ell_1$ quantile-regression estimator studied by \citet{fu2000asymptotics} and \citet{belloni2011}. It is a robust prediction method in high-dimensional linear models.

\begin{assumption}[Linear target]\label{ass_hd}
\begin{revision}
The conditional quantile is $f^*(x)=x^\top\beta^*$ for some $\beta^*\in\mathbb R^d$ with $\|\beta^*\|_1\le V$, and the functions $x\mapsto x^\top\beta$ in the constraint set satisfy the common bound \eqref{F_2}.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{revision}
Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal H(V)=\{x\mapsto x^\top\beta:\|\beta\|_1\le V\},
 \qquad
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal H(V)}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{QR_est}
\end{equation}
For block $I=\mathcal J_{a,\ell}$, let $X_I^w$ be the matrix whose row corresponding to $(i,j)$ is $x_{ij}^\top/m_i$, and set
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_n=\sum_{a\in\{e,o\}}\sum_{\ell=1}^L
 \mathbb E\max_{q\in[d]}\|X^w_{a,\ell,\cdot q}\|_2.       \label{eq:An_linear_full}
\end{equation}
\label{eq:An_linear}
\end{revision}

\subsection{Result for High-dimensional Quantile Linear Regression}
\begin{theorem}[High-dimensional linear prediction]\label{thm_hd}
\begin{revision}
Under Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} and Assumption~\ref{ass_hd},
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[
 b_B^2\left\{\frac{V\sqrt{\log(2d)}}{n}\mathcal A_n+\Gamma_n\right\}
 \right].                                                     \label{eq:hd_rate_full}
\end{equation}
If the unweighted block columns have Euclidean norm $O(\sqrt{M_{a,\ell}})$ and $m_i\asymp m$, the stochastic term is of order $V\sqrt{L\log d/N}$ up to constants. This is a prediction bound. Estimation of $\beta^*$ or variable-selection consistency requires an additional restricted-eigenvalue or identifiability condition.
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}


\section{Application To Quantile \( K \)-NN fused lasso}

For $d>1$, we consider a quantile version of the $K$-nearest-neighbor graph fused lasso \citep{elmoataz2008nonlocal,ferradans2014regularized,madrid2020adaptive}. Construct the symmetric union $K$-NN graph $G_K=(V_K,E_K)$ on the $N$ observed design points: an undirected edge is included when either endpoint is among the other's $K$ nearest neighbors.

\begin{revision}
For $\theta\in\mathbb R^N$, let $D_{G_K}$ be an oriented incidence matrix and define
\begin{equation}
 \|D_{G_K}\theta\|_1=\sum_{(u,v)\in E_K}|\theta_u-\theta_v|. \label{tv_knn}
\end{equation}
\label{def_knn}
For a piecewise-Lipschitz target, the natural graph-TV scale is
\begin{equation}
 \|D_{G_K}\theta^*\|_1\lesssim N^{1-1/d}\operatorname{polylog}(N), \label{l1_norm_for_knn_reg}
\end{equation}
not $N^{-1/d}$. The latter is the scale of the prediction risk, not the unnormalized graph total variation.
\end{revision}

\begin{assumption}[Graph geometry and target regularity]\label{ass_knn}
\begin{revision}
The design density is bounded above and below on $[0,1]^d$, $d>1$, and $K$ is polylogarithmic in $N$. On an event whose probability tends to one: (i) the symmetric union $K$-NN graph is connected; (ii) with $D_{G_K}^{\dagger}$ the Moore--Penrose inverse of its incidence matrix,
\[
 \rho(G_K):=\max_{e\in E_K}\|(D_{G_K}^{\dagger})_{\cdot e}\|_2\le\rho_N;
\]
(iii) the $K$-NN radius is at most $C_R(K/N)^{1/d}$; (iv) the neighbor-overlap bound
$Q\{x:x_v\in N_K(x)\}\le C_{\mathrm O}K/N$ holds for every sampled vertex $v$; and (v), writing $r_N=C_R(K/N)^{1/d}$ and $\mathcal S$ for the singularity set, the empirical boundary-tube count satisfies $N^{-1}\sum_{v=1}^N\mathbf 1\{x_v\in B_{r_N}(\mathcal S)\}\le C_{\mathcal S}r_N$. The target is bounded and piecewise Lipschitz with a singularity set whose $r$-neighborhood has $Q$-measure $O(r)$, and $V_n\ge\|D_{G_K}\theta^*\|_1$. Lemma~\ref{app:lem:graph-tv-scale} shows that one may take $V_n\lesssim N^{1-1/d}K^{1+1/d}$ on the stated geometry event.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\subsection{Result for Quantile \( K \)-NN fused lasso}
\begin{revision}
The constrained in-sample estimator is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat\theta\in\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb R^N}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau(y_{ij}-\theta_{ij})
 \quad\text{subject to }\|D_{G_K}\theta\|_1\le V_n.          \label{np_knn1}
\end{equation}
Set $\widehat f(x_{ij})=\widehat\theta_{ij}$ at sampled points and, for a new $x$, define
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f(x)=\frac1{|N_K(x)|}\sum_{x_{ij}\in N_K(x)}\widehat\theta_{ij}. \label{np_knn2}
\end{equation}
\end{revision}

\begin{theorem}[Quantile $K$-NN fused lasso]\label{Theorem_5}
\begin{revision}
Suppose Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} and Assumption~\ref{ass_knn} hold. Then
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat\theta-\theta^*\|_{n,m}^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[b_B^2\left\{
 \frac{L}{N}+
 \frac{LV_n\rho_N\sqrt{\log(2|E_K|)}}{N}
 +\Gamma_n\right\}\right],                                   \label{knn_loss_1}
\end{equation}
and the nearest-neighbor extension satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[b_B^2\left\{
 \frac{L}{N}+
 \frac{LV_n\rho_N\sqrt{\log(2|E_K|)}}{N}
 +\Gamma_n+
 \left(\frac{K}{N}\right)^{1/d}\right\}\right].             \label{eq:knn_function_rate_full}
\end{equation}
If $\rho_N$ and $K$ are polylogarithmic and
$V_n\lesssim N^{1-1/d}K^{1+1/d}$, the leading nonparametric term is
$N^{-1/d}$ up to logarithmic factors and $\Gamma_n$. Proposition~\ref{app:prop:graph-complexity} proves the localized graph-complexity inequality used here.
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}


\section{For $\mathcal F$ as a Non-convex Set and $f^*\notin\mathcal F$}

\begin{revision}
For a possibly nonconvex class $\mathcal F$, define the centered star hull
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Star}(\mathcal F-f^*)
 =\{\mu(f-f^*):f\in\mathcal F,\ \mu\in[0,1]\}.               \label{star_shaped}
\end{equation}
Choose a function $f^\circ\in\mathcal F$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
 \|f^\circ-f^*\|_\infty
 \le\inf_{f\in\mathcal F}\|f-f^*\|_\infty+n^{-1},
 \qquad \phi_n=\|f^\circ-f^*\|_\infty.                      \label{eq:nonconvex_projection_full}
\end{equation}
This is a function-valued near-best approximation; the scalar infimum used in the supplied manuscript was not a projection.
\end{revision}

\begin{theorem}[Nonconvex temporal-spatial quantile regression]\label{Theorem_non_convex}
\begin{revision}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{Assumption 1.}, \ref{assumptionA}, \ref{assump:lip}, and \ref{ass:tail_multi_d} hold, and $f^*$ and all $f\in\mathcal F$ satisfy \eqref{F_2}. Let
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal F}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{main_est_nonconvex}
\end{equation}
Define the critical radius as in \eqref{eq:critical_radius}, with $\mathcal H_{\mathcal F}(r)$ replaced by the localized centered star hull. Then
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\{b_B^2(r_n^2+\phi_n^2)\}.                    \label{eq:nonconvex_rate_full}
\end{equation}
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}

\begin{revision}
The proof controls the absolute value of one centered localized empirical process, rather than duplicating twelve one-sided decompositions. The approximation-adjusted radius is used consistently throughout.
\end{revision}


\section{Application to Neural Networks}

Deep ReLU networks provide flexible nonparametric estimators and can exploit hierarchical composition structure to mitigate the ambient-dimensional curse \citep{schmidhuber2015deep,schmidt2020nonparametric,kohler2019rate}.

\begin{revision}
For depth $L$, width $r$, sparsity $S$, and clipping level $B$, let
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}}(L,r,S,B)
 =\{\,\operatorname{clip}_B(f):\;&f\text{ is a ReLU network with depth }L,\\
 &\text{width at most }r,\text{ and at most }S\text{ nonzero parameters}\,\}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:space_of_networks}
\end{equation}
The clipping enforces \eqref{F_2}; the sparsity restriction makes the entropy finite and explicit.
\end{revision}

\begin{definition}[Hierarchical composition model]\label{def_h_c_model}
\begin{revision}
Let $\mathcal P\subset(0,\infty)\times\mathbb N$ be finite. A level-zero component is a coordinate projection. Recursively, a level-$(\ell+1)$ component is $g(f_1,\ldots,f_K)$, where $(p,K)\in\mathcal P$, $g:\mathbb R^K\to\mathbb R$ is $(p,C)$-smooth on the relevant bounded domain, and each $f_k$ is a level-$\ell$ component. The target $f^*$ belongs to a fixed-level hierarchical composition class of this form.
\end{revision}
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Network approximation, entropy, and balance]\label{assump_nn}
\begin{revision}
There are architecture sequences $(L_N,r_N,S_N)$ and $f_N^\circ\in\mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}}(L_N,r_N,S_N,B)$ such that, with
\[
 a_N^2=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal P}N^{-2p/(2p+K)}\operatorname{polylog}(N),
\]
we have $\|f_N^\circ-f^*\|_\infty^2\lesssim a_N^2$ and, uniformly over empirical measures,
\[
 \log N\{\epsilon,\mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}}(L_N,r_N,S_N,B),\|\cdot\|_{n,m}\}
 \lesssim S_NL_N\log(2S_N)\log(CB/\epsilon).
\]
The architecture is selected so that
\[
 \frac{S_NL_N\log(2S_N)\,L\log N}{N}\lesssim a_N^2.
\]
These three displayed conditions isolate the approximation theorem, the entropy theorem, and their required balance; the proof uses no unspecified logarithmic exponent beyond the one encoded in $a_N$.
\end{revision}
\end{assumption}

\begin{revision}
The estimator is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}}(L_N,r_N,S_N,B)}
 \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}.                              \label{nn_main_est}
\end{equation}
\end{revision}

\subsection{Results For Neural Networks}
\begin{theorem}[Neural-network quantile regression]\label{Theorem_nn_rate}
\begin{revision}
Under Theorem~\ref{Theorem_non_convex}, Assumption~\ref{assump_nn}, and the hierarchical composition condition in Definition~\ref{def_h_c_model},
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[
 b_B^2\left\{
 \max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal P}N^{-2p/(2p+K)}\operatorname{polylog}(N)
 +\Gamma_n\right\}\right].                                  \label{nn_rate}
\end{equation}
The exponent depends on the local input dimension $K$ of the composition components rather than on the ambient dimension $d$.
\end{revision}
\end{theorem}



\section{Algorithms}

\begin{revision}
We use scaled-form ADMM. The supplied dual ``argmax'' was unbounded; the correct scaled-dual step is an additive update.
\end{revision}

\subsection{Quantile Trend Filtering}
\begin{revision}
Let $M=N$, let $\widetilde x_1\le\cdots\le\widetilde x_M$ be the ordered univariate design points, and let $D^{(k)}_{\widetilde x}$ denote the standard order-$k$ trend-filtering difference operator on unevenly spaced inputs. The penalized problem is
\begin{equation}
 \min_{\theta\in\mathbb R^M}
 \sum_{i,j}w_{ij}\rho_\tau(y_{ij}-\theta_{ij})
 +\lambda\|D^{(k)}_{\widetilde x}\theta\|_1,
 \qquad w_{ij}=\frac1{nm_i}.                                 \label{70}
\end{equation}
With the split $\theta=z$, the scaled updates are
\begin{align}
 \theta^{t+1}&=\argmin_\theta
 \sum_{i,j}w_{ij}\rho_\tau(y_{ij}-\theta_{ij})
 +\frac\varrho2\|\theta-z^t+u^t\|_2^2,                     \label{71}\\
 z^{t+1}&=\argmin_z\frac12\|\theta^{t+1}+u^t-z\|_2^2
 +\frac\lambda\varrho\|D^{(k)}_{\widetilde x}z\|_1,       \label{72}\\
 u^{t+1}&=u^t+\theta^{t+1}-z^{t+1}.                           \label{73}
\end{align}
The first update is the weighted check-loss proximal map. For $a=z^t_{ij}-u^t_{ij}$,
\begin{equation}
 \theta^{t+1}_{ij}=
 \begin{cases}
 a+w_{ij}\tau/\varrho,&y_{ij}>a+w_{ij}\tau/\varrho,\\
 a-w_{ij}(1-\tau)/\varrho,&y_{ij}<a-w_{ij}(1-\tau)/\varrho,\\
 y_{ij},&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}                                                  \label{74}
\end{equation}
\end{revision}

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{ADMM for quantile trend filtering}\label{alg_tf_admm}
\KwIn{$y$, ordered design points, $\lambda$, $\tau$, $\varrho$, tolerance $\kappa$, maximum iteration count.}
Initialize $\theta^0=z^0=y$ and $u^0=0$\;
\For{$t=0,1,2,\ldots$ until the primal and dual residual criteria are met}{
Update $\theta^{t+1}$ coordinatewise using \eqref{74}\;
Update $z^{t+1}$ by the trend-filtering proximal problem \eqref{72}\;
Update $u^{t+1}=u^t+\theta^{t+1}-z^{t+1}$\;
}
\KwOut{$\widehat\theta=\theta^{t+1}$.}
\end{algorithm}

\subsection{Quantile K-NN Fused Lasso}
\begin{revision}
For the graph problem, replace $D^{(k)}_{\widetilde x}$ by the graph incidence matrix $D_{G_K}$:
\begin{align}
 \theta^{t+1}&=\argmin_\theta
 \sum_{i,j}w_{ij}\rho_\tau(y_{ij}-\theta_{ij})
 +\frac\varrho2\|\theta-z^t+u^t\|_2^2,                     \label{82}\\
 z^{t+1}&=\argmin_z\frac12\|\theta^{t+1}+u^t-z\|_2^2
 +\frac\lambda\varrho\|D_{G_K}z\|_1,                      \label{83}\\
 u^{t+1}&=u^t+\theta^{t+1}-z^{t+1}.                           \label{84}
\end{align}
The $z$ update is a graph total-variation proximal problem.
\end{revision}

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{ADMM for quantile $K$-NN graph fused lasso}\label{alg_knn_admm}
\KwIn{$y$, design points, $K$, $\lambda$, $\tau$, $\varrho$, tolerance $\kappa$, maximum iteration count.}
Construct the symmetric union $K$-NN graph and its incidence matrix $D_{G_K}$\;
Initialize $\theta^0=z^0=y$ and $u^0=0$\;
\For{$t=0,1,2,\ldots$ until the primal and dual residual criteria are met}{
Update $\theta^{t+1}$ coordinatewise using the weighted check-loss proximal map\;
Update $z^{t+1}$ by \eqref{83}\;
Update $u^{t+1}=u^t+\theta^{t+1}-z^{t+1}$\;
}
\KwOut{$\widehat\theta=\theta^{t+1}$.}
\end{algorithm}


\section{Experiments}
\begin{revision}The experimental designs, numerical values, tuning grids, and substantive interpretations are retained. Edits in this section are limited to grammar, notation, and reproducibility statements; no new experimental result is inserted.\end{revision}

This section provides numerical evidence to support our theoretical results. We start by conducting empirical experiments to evaluate the performance of the penalized estimators for Trend Filtering, High Dimensional Quantile Regression and \( K \)-NN-FL, respectively, for both settings \( d = 1 \) and \( d > 1 \). 

To establish a comparison, we include various competing methods as benchmarks. 

\subsection{Simulated data}
\label{subsec:simulated_data}
To conduct our simulations, we consider various generative models, referred to as scenarios, for both settings \( d = 1 \) and \( d > 1 \). We describe these scenarios in the following subsections. Next, we outline the common aspects of our simulations for both the univariate and multivariate settings.

For the $\tau = 0.5$ setting, the performance of each method is evaluated by the MSE obtained when using the true quantile function. We then report the average MSE over 50 Monte Carlo simulations. For other $\tau$, we evaluate the performance using the test set coverage, averaged over 5 train-test splits. In both cases, we then report the average metric over 50 Monte Carlo simulations. 

For each setting below, we vary \( n \) in in \( \{100, 200, 400\} \). For each fix value \( n \) we then vary a parameter \( m_{mult} \) in \( \{0.5, 1, 2\} \), to obtain different sets of values for \( \{m_i\}_{i=1}^{n} \).


 Specifically, we consider:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \( m_1 = \ldots = m_{n/4} = 16m_{mult} \),
    \item \( m_{n/4+1} = \ldots = m_{n/2} = 24m_{mult} \),
    \item \( m_{n/2+1} = \ldots = m_{3n/4} = 20m_{mult} \),
    \item \( m_{3n/4+1} = \ldots = m_n = 10m_{mult} \).
\end{itemize}



\rev{The spatial effect is generated as} 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_delta_def}
\delta_i(x) \,=\, 0.5\delta_{i-1}(x) + \sum_{t=1}^{25} t^{-1}b_{i,t}h_t(x), 
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:h_t_def}
h_t(x) \,=\, \left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi \sin(tx(j))\right)_{j=1}^{25},
\end{equation}
are basis functions and $b_{i,t}\sim G_i$ for some distributions $G_i$. The measurement error is generated as follows: For each \(i \in [n]\), the vector \(\tilde e_i \in \mathbb{R}^M\) is generated recursively as, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_e_def}
\tilde e_i = 0.3 \tilde e_{i-1} + \xi_i,
\end{equation}
where \(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\) are independent errors with $\xi_i \sim F_i$ for some distributions $F_i$. To form the measurement errors, for each \(i \in [n]\), we take the first \(m_i\) entries of the vector \(\tilde e_i\) and denote the resulting vector as \(e_i\), since $m_i$ are varied across $i$. We note, also, that when $\tau \neq 0.5$, a normalization factor is included for both the spatial noise and measurement error to ensure the variance remains constant across $i$. 


\rev{We observe the noisy spatio-temporal data} \(\{y_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_i}\) at design points \(\{x_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_1}\) evenly sampled from \(\text{Unif}([0,1]^d)\) as follows. First we generate \(\{x_{1,j}\}_{j=1}^{m_1} \sim \text{Unif}([0,1]^d)\), then for any \(1 < i \,\leq\, n\),

\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_xij}
x_{ij} = 
\begin{cases}
x_{i-1,j}, & \text{with probability } \phi = 0.1,\\
x_{ij} \sim \text{Unif}([0,1]^d), & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
for all \(j = 1, \ldots, m_i\).





\subsection{Univariate Study}

For quantile trend filtering, we consider tuning parameters $\lambda$ such that $\log_{10}(\lambda)$ is in a grid of 50 evenly spaced points between $-7$ and $1$, where $a, b\in \R$. We then perform Algorithm 1 on $\lambda/M$. 

\rev{To select $\lambda$, we use} Bayesian Information Criteria (BIC; cite{schwarz1978estimating}). The BIC for quantile regression cite{yu2001bayesian} can be computed as
\[
\text{BIC}(\tau) \,=\, \frac{2}{\sigma} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \rho_{\tau}(y_{i} - \hat{\theta}_{i}) + \nu \log n,
\]
where $\nu$ denotes the degrees of freedom of the estimator, and $\sigma > 0$ can be empirically chosen as $\sigma = 1 - |1 - 2\tau|$. We estimate $\nu$ by the $d + k + 1$, where $d$ is the number of elements of the k-th order difference operator larger than 0.05 in absolute value.  

As benchmark methods, we consider the usual (mean regression) penalized trend filtering estimator of order $k = 1$ and $k = 2$ denoted as TF1 and TF2 respectively, and quantile cubic smoothing splines, denoted as QSS. Note that TF1 and TF2 only provide estimates for $\tau = 0.5$. 

Mean regression trend filtering is implemented using the \texttt{trendfilter} function in the \texttt{glmgen} package, and the tuning parameter selected via BIC. 

\rev{Quantile smoothing splines are implemented} with a similar method as Algorithm~\ref{alg_tf_admm}, with step (ii) replaced by the \texttt{smooth.spline} function in the \texttt{fields} package. A full derivation of this Algorithm is presented in the appendix. The tuning parameter is selected via $K$-fold cross validation. 

\rev{For all of the following scenarios}, we consider \( f^* = f - \int_{0}^{1} f(x)dx \) for the following \rev{sinusoidal} function, which is constructed to have with \rev{nonhomogeneous} smoothness:
\[
    f(x) \,=\, 
    2x^{\frac{1}{4}} \sin \left( \frac{2\pi}{(x+0.1)^{\frac{1}{2}}} \right)
\]

% \begin{enumerate}

% \item Function 1 (Doppler-Spatially-Varying Frequency).
%     \[
%     f_1(x) = 
%     2x^{\frac{1}{4}} \sin \left( \frac{2\pi}{(x+0.1)^{\frac{1}{2}}} \right)
%     \]

% \item Function 2 (Piece-wise linear).
%     \[
%    f_2(x) = 
%     \begin{cases}
%     2.5x & \text{if } x \leq 0.4, \\
%     45x - 17 & \text{if } 0.4 < x \leq 0.6, \\
%     -40x + 34 & \text{if } 0.6 < x \leq 0.8, \\
%     30x - 22 & \text{if } 0.8 < x.
%     \end{cases}
%     \]

% \item Function 3
% (Lagrange Polynomial).
%     \[
%     f_3(x) = -\frac{2125}{6}x^4 + \frac{2050}{3}x^3 - \frac{2465}{6}x^2 + \frac{248}{3}x.
%     \]
    
% \end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}
    
\item \textbf{Scenario 1}: We take $G_i\sim N(0, 1)$ and $F_i\sim N(0, 0.5)$ This scenario may serve as a baseline. 

\item \textbf{Scenario 2}: We take $G_i\sim \text{laplace}(0, 2)$ and $F_i\sim t(3)$. Here $t(3)$ denotes the t-distribution with 3 degrees of freedom. 

% \item \textbf{Scenario 3}: We take $f(x) = f_2(x)$,  $G_i\sim N(0, 3)$, and $F_i\sim \text{laplace}(0, 2)$. 
 
% \item \textbf{Scenario 4}: We take $f(x) = f_3(x)$,  $G_i\sim t(3)$, and $F_i\sim N(0, 1)$. 

\item \textbf{Scenario 3}: We take $G_i\sim N(0, 1)$, and $F_i\sim N(0, 1)$. However, here we consider $\tau = 0.2$ and $\tau = 0.8$. 

\end{enumerate}

Other scenarios, with other underlying functions, are demonstrated in the Appendix.

\subsection{Multivariate Study}
\rev{For all of the following scenarios}, we consider \( f^* = f - \int_{0}^{1} f(x)dx \) for the following piecewise constant function:
\[
f_0(x) =
\begin{cases} 
1 & \text{if } \|x - \frac{1}{4} \mathbf{1}_d\|_2 < \| x - \frac{3}{4} \mathbf{1}_d \|_2, \\
-1 & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\]

\begin{enumerate}
    
\item \textbf{Scenario 1}: We take $G_i\sim N(0, 1)$, $F_i\sim N(0, 0.5)$, and $d=2$. This scenario may serve as a baseline. 

\item \textbf{Scenario 2}: We take $G_i\sim t(2)$, $F_i\sim \text{cauchy}(0, 1)$, and $d=2$. $t(2)$ denotes the t-distribution with 2 degrees of freedom. 

\item \textbf{Scenario 3}: We take $G_i\sim N(0, 1)$, $F_i\sim N(0, 1)$, and consider $\tau = 0.2$ and $\tau = 0.8$ $d=2$. 

% However, we also scale the measurement errors $\tilde e_{ij}$ by $\|x_{ij}\|_2$, so the quantiles of the measurement errors correlate with the inputs. We consider $\tau = 0.2$ and $\tau = 0.8$, with the necessary predicted values for the coverage calculation acquired using 1-NN interpolation. 

\item \textbf{Scenario 4}: We take $G_i\sim N(0, 2)$, and $F_i\sim t(3)$. However, here we consider $d = 5$ and $d = 10$. 

\end{enumerate}


\subsection{Non-Convex Study}

\rev{We also study a nonconvex setting} by replacing the convex estimators in the multivariate study with neural networks. 
We consider two neural-network baselines with identical architectures, differing only in the training loss:
(i) a squared-error network (SqErr Network) trained by minimizing the mean squared error loss, and 
(ii) a quantile network (Quantile Network) trained by minimizing the pinball loss for a set of target quantile levels 
$\mathcal{Q}=\{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7,0.9\}$.


We consider three spatial-temporal scenarios (Scenarios~6--8) in the multivariate setting with $d=2$.
All three scenarios follow the common sampling scheme described in Section~\ref{subsec:simulated_data} (Simulated data), 
including the heterogeneous per-time sample sizes $\{m_i\}_{i=1}^n$, the temporally dependent design points $\{x_{ij}\}$, 
and the spatio-temporal noise components $\delta_i(\cdot)$ and $e_i$.
Specifically, for each $i\in[n]$ and $j\in[m_i]$, the response is generated as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_yij}
y_{ij} \;=\; \beta(x_{ij}) \;+\; \delta_i(x_{ij}) \;+\; e_{ij},
\end{equation}
where the signal $\beta(\cdot)$ is the piecewise-constant function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_beta_piecewise}
\beta(x)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \text{if } \left\|x-\frac14\mathbf{1}_d\right\|_2 < \left\|x-\frac34\mathbf{1}_d\right\|_2,\\
-1, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Design points and flattening.}
For a fixed temporal length $n$, we first generate a padded array $\{x_{i\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^M$ with $M=\max_i m_i$ by sampling 
$x_{1\ell}\stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}([0,1]^d)$ and then, for $i\,\ge\, 2$, copying each previous location with probability $\phi=0.1$ 
(as in \eqref{eq:st_xij} in Section~\ref{subsec:simulated_data}); we only keep the first $m_i$ locations at time $i$, i.e., $\{x_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_i}$.
The training set used by neural networks is obtained by flattening the panel data
$\{(x_{ij},y_{ij})\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_i}$ into i.i.d.-style pairs:
\[
X_{\mathrm{train}}=\big[x_{11};\ldots;x_{1m_1};x_{21};\ldots;x_{nm_n}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{(\sum_i m_i)\times d}, 
\qquad 
y_{\mathrm{train}}=\big[y_{11},\ldots,y_{nm_n}\big]^\top\in\mathbb{R}^{\sum_i m_i}.
\]
The test set is formed in the same way using an independently generated panel of design points.

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Spatial noise.}
The spatio-temporal component $\delta_i(\cdot)$ follows the recursive construction in \eqref{eq:st_delta_def}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_delta_nn}
\delta_i(x)\;=\;0.5\,\delta_{i-1}(x)\;+\;\sum_{t=1}^{25} t^{-1} b_{i,t}\, h_t(x),
\qquad b_{i,t}\sim G_i,
\end{equation}
where $\{h_t\}_{t=1}^{25}$ are sine-basis functions as in~\eqref{eq:h_t_def}. 
In implementation, for each location $x=(x(1),\ldots,x(d))\in[0,1]^d$, we use
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_ht}
h_t(x)\;=\;\prod_{r=1}^d \left(\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}\sin\big(t\,x(r)\big)\right),\qquad t=1,\ldots,25.
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Measurement error.}
The measurement error is generated via the AR-type recursion in \eqref{eq:st_e_def}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st_e_nn}
\tilde e_i \;=\; 0.3\,\tilde e_{i-1} \;+\; \xi_i,\qquad \xi_i\sim F_i,
\end{equation}
where $\tilde e_i\in\mathbb{R}^M$, and we set $e_{ij}$ to be the $j$th entry of $\tilde e_i$ for $j\,\le\, m_i$.

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Scenario specifications.}
The scenarios differ only through the innovation distributions $G_i$ and $F_i$ and the quantile level $\tau$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Scenario 6 (light-tailed):} $b_{i,t}\stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,1)$ and $\xi_i(\ell)\stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,0.5^2)$.
    \item \textbf{Scenario 7 (heavy-tailed):} $b_{i,t}\stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}t(5)$ and $\xi_i(\ell)\stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}t(5)$, leading to heavier tails in both $\delta_i(\cdot)$ and $e_i$.
    \item \textbf{Scenario 8 
    % ($\tau\neq 0.5$ with variance-stabilized recursion)
    :}
we consider $\tau=0.5$ and define the target quantile signal as 
$\beta_\tau(x)=f_0(x)+\Phi^{-1}(\tau)$.
The spatial-noise coefficients follow an AR-type recursion:
for $t=1,\ldots,25$, let $z_{i,t}\stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,1)$ and set
\[
b_{i,t}=\frac{0.5\,b_{i-1,t}+t^{-1}z_{i,t}}{s_\delta},
\qquad s_\delta=\sqrt{0.5^2+1^2},
\]
(with $s_\delta=1$ at $i=1$), and define $\delta_i(x)=\sum_{t=1}^{25} b_{i,t}h_t(x)$.
The measurement error is generated by
$u_{i,\ell}\stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}t(3)$ and
\[
\tilde e_i(\ell)=\frac{0.3\,\tilde e_{i-1}(\ell)+u_{i,\ell}}{s_e},
\qquad s_e=\sqrt{0.3^2+1^2},
\]
(with $s_e=1$ at $i=1$), and we take $e_{ij}=\tilde e_i(j)$ for $j\,\le\, m_i$.
Finally, $y_{ij}=\beta_\tau(x_{ij})+\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}$.
\end{itemize}

% \paragraph{Data generating scenarios.}
% We focus on three spatial-temporal scenarios (Scenarios 6--8) with $d=2$ and temporal dependence in both the spatial noise and measurement error.
% The true signal is the piecewise-constant function 
% \[
% f_0(x)=
% \begin{cases} 
% 1 & \text{if } \|x - \frac{1}{4}\mathbf{1}_d\|_2 < \|x - \frac{3}{4}\mathbf{1}_d\|_2,\\
% -1 & \text{otherwise}.
% \end{cases}
% \]
% The spatial noise is generated by a sine-basis expansion with temporal dependence, and the measurement error follows an AR-type recursion.
% Scenario 6 uses Gaussian innovations (light-tailed), Scenario 7 uses heavy-tailed innovations, and Scenario 8 considers $\tau\neq 0.5$ with a normalization to keep the variance stable across time indices.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\rev{Evaluation metric.}}\ 
For each quantile level $q\in\mathcal{Q}$, we evaluate the squared error between the learned quantile function and the oracle quantile function on a large test set, and report the average over Monte Carlo trials.
We vary the temporal length $n\in\{100,500,1000\}$ (with the corresponding $\{m_i\}$ pattern described above).

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\rev{Summary of findings.}\ }
Table~\ref{tab:nn_nonconvex} shows that the Quantile Network consistently outperforms the SqErr Network across all three scenarios and all quantile levels.
The improvements are particularly pronounced for tail quantiles ($q=0.1$ and $q=0.9$), where minimizing squared error is misaligned with the quantile target and heavy-tailed errors exacerbate the discrepancy.
In contrast, the pinball loss directly targets the conditional quantiles and yields substantially smaller errors, especially in Scenarios 6 and 8.
