\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}}
\label{app_b}

This appendix gives a complete proof of the convex theorem using the same
weights as the empirical criterion.  In particular, all dependence is handled
at the level of the joint time cluster
\[
  W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i),\qquad
  X_i=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{im_i}),\quad
  E_i=(e_{i1},\ldots,e_{im_i}),
\]
and every empirical process is centered at $f^*$.  No separate coupling of
$X_i$, $\delta_i$, and $E_i$ is used.

\subsection{Losses, weights, and local classes}

Write
\[
  N=\sum_{i=1}^n m_i,\qquad w_{ij}=\frac{1}{nm_i},
  \qquad h_f=f-f^*.
\]
Assumption~\ref{Assumption 1.}\ref{asm:m} implies that there are constants
$0<c_w<C_w<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
  \frac{c_w}{N}\le w_{ij}\le \frac{C_w}{N}
  \quad\text{for every }(i,j).                                  \label{app:eq:balanced-weights}
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{align}
  \ell_{ij,f}
  &=\rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}
    -\rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f^*(x_{ij})\},                           \label{app:eq:loss-diff}\\
  \widehat Z(f)&=\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}w_{ij}\ell_{ij,f},
  \qquad \overline Z(f)=\mathbb E\widehat Z(f).                \label{app:eq:emp-pop-loss}
\end{align}
For $t>0$, define the Huber-localized and $L_2(Q)$-localized classes
\begin{align}
  \mathcal F_\Delta(t)
  &=\{f\in\mathcal F:c_0\Delta^2(h_f)\le t^2\},                \label{app:eq:Fdelta}\\
  \mathcal H_2(r)
  &=\{h_f:f\in\mathcal F,\ \|h_f\|_2\le r\}.                 \label{app:eq:H2}
\end{align}
The classes used below are centered because they contain $h_{f^*}=0$.

\begin{lemma}[Knight identity and curvature]
\label{app:lem:curvature}
Under Assumption~\ref{assumptionA}, for every measurable $f$ satisfying
$\|f\|_\infty\le B$,
\begin{equation}
  \overline Z(f)\ge C_0\Delta^2(h_f),\qquad
  C_0=\underline f\min\left\{\frac12,\frac R4\right\}.          \label{app:eq:curvature}
\end{equation}
Moreover, if $\|h_f\|_\infty\le R$, then
\begin{equation}
  \overline Z(f)\le \frac{\overline f}{2}\|h_f\|_2^2.         \label{app:eq:upper-curvature}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $U_{ij}=y_{ij}-f^*(x_{ij})$ and $h=h_f(x_{ij})$.  Knight's identity gives
\[
 \rho_\tau(U_{ij}-h)-\rho_\tau(U_{ij})
 =-h\{\tau-\mathbbm 1(U_{ij}\le0)\}
   +\int_0^h\{\mathbbm 1(U_{ij}\le u)-\mathbbm 1(U_{ij}\le0)\}\,du.
\]
After conditioning on $x_{ij}=x$, the linear term vanishes because
$\mathbb P(U_{ij}\le0\mid x_{ij}=x)=\tau$.  Thus
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E(\ell_{ij,f}\mid x_{ij}=x)
 =\int_0^{h_f(x)}
 \{F_{U,ij}(u\mid x)-F_{U,ij}(0\mid x)\}\,du.                  \label{app:eq:knight-conditional}
\end{equation}
If $|h_f(x)|\le R$, Assumption~\ref{assumptionA} bounds this integral below by
$\underline f h_f(x)^2/2$.  If $h_f(x)>R$, monotonicity of the conditional CDF
and the same assumption at $R/2$ imply
\[
 \int_0^{h_f(x)}\{F_{U,ij}(u\mid x)-F_{U,ij}(0\mid x)\}\,du
 \ge \frac{\underline f R}{2}\{h_f(x)-R/2\}
 \ge \frac{\underline f R}{4}|h_f(x)|.
\]
The case $h_f(x)<-R$ follows by reversing the direction of integration.  Since
$|z|\ge z^2$ when $|z|\le1$, these bounds imply
\[
 \mathbb E(\ell_{ij,f}\mid x_{ij}=x)
 \ge C_0\min\{|h_f(x)|,h_f(x)^2\}.
\]
Integrating over $Q$ and averaging with the weights $w_{ij}$ proves
\eqref{app:eq:curvature}.  The upper bound follows from the upper inequality in
Assumption~\ref{assumptionA} applied to \eqref{app:eq:knight-conditional}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Comparison with squared prediction loss]
\label{app:lem:norm-comparison}
If $\|f\|_\infty,\|f^*\|_\infty\le B$, then
\begin{equation}
  \|h_f\|_2^2\le b_B^2\Delta^2(h_f),
  \qquad b_B^2=1\vee 2B.                                      \label{app:eq:norm-comparison}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
  \{h_f:f\in\mathcal F_\Delta(t)\}
  \subseteq \mathcal H_2(c_0^{-1/2}b_Bt).                     \label{app:eq:local-inclusion}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Pointwise, $|h_f|\le2B$ and
$h_f^2\le(1\vee2B)\min\{|h_f|,h_f^2\}$.  Integrating proves
\eqref{app:eq:norm-comparison}; \eqref{app:eq:local-inclusion} follows
immediately.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Interlaced blocks and joint-cluster coupling}

For an integer $L\ge1$ and $\ell\in[L]$, define
\begin{align}
 \mathcal J_{e,\ell}
 &=\{\ell+2qL:q\ge0\}\cap[n],                                  \label{app:eq:block-even}\\
 \mathcal J_{o,\ell}
 &=\{\ell+(2q+1)L:q\ge0\}\cap[n].                              \label{app:eq:block-odd}
\end{align}
These $2L$ sets partition $[n]$, and consecutive indices within a fixed set
are separated by $2L$.  Under Assumption~\ref{Assumption 1.}\ref{ass_c_beta},
we take
\begin{equation}
  L=L_n=\left\lceil\frac{2}{C_\beta}\log n\right\rceil.        \label{app:eq:block-length}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Joint Berbee coupling]
\label{app:lem:joint-coupling}
On an enlarged probability space, for every block
$I\in\{\mathcal J_{e,\ell},\mathcal J_{o,\ell}:\ell\in[L]\}$
there are random elements $\{W_i^*:i\in I\}$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item $W_i^*$ has the same marginal law as $W_i$;
\item the variables $\{W_i^*:i\in I\}$ are mutually independent;
\item
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\{W_i\ne W_i^*\text{ for some }i\in I\}
 \le (|I|-1)\beta_W(2L).                                      \label{app:eq:block-coupling-error}
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
If $\Omega_\beta$ denotes the event that all coupled blocks agree with their
original blocks, then
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(\Omega_\beta^c)\le n\beta_W(2L)=O(n^{-3}).          \label{app:eq:total-coupling-error}
\end{equation}
The coupled copies need not be independent across different blocks; the proof
only uses independence within one block at a time.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply Berbee's coupling lemma successively to the subsequence
$\{W_i:i\in I\}$.  Its consecutive elements are separated by $2L$, which gives
\eqref{app:eq:block-coupling-error}.  Summing the block errors yields at most
$n\beta_W(2L)$.  The exponential mixing bound and
\eqref{app:eq:block-length} give the last order statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Conditional decomposition of the empirical process}

For each $(i,j)$ and $f$, define
\begin{align}
 \widetilde Z_{ij}(f)
 &=\mathbb E(\ell_{ij,f}\mid X_i,\delta_i),                     \label{app:eq:tildeZ}\\
 Z_{ij}(f)
 &=\mathbb E(\ell_{ij,f}\mid x_{ij}),                           \label{app:eq:Z}\\
 \overline Z_{ij}(f)&=\mathbb E\ell_{ij,f}.                    \label{app:eq:barZ}
\end{align}
The distinction between conditioning on $(X_i,\delta_i)$ and conditioning on
$x_{ij}$ is essential.

\begin{lemma}[Exact spatial-effect identity]
\label{app:lem:exact-spatial}
Let
\begin{equation}
 D_i(x,u)=\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid x_{i1}=x)
 -\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid\delta_i,x_{i1}=x),                 \label{app:eq:Di}
\end{equation}
where $U_{ij}=\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 Z_{ij}(f)-\widetilde Z_{ij}(f)
 =r_{ij}(f):=\int_0^{h_f(x_{ij})}D_i(x_{ij},u)\,du.             \label{app:eq:rij-exact}
\end{equation}
For every fixed $(x,\delta_i)$, the map $z\mapsto\int_0^zD_i(x,u)du$ is
one-Lipschitz and vanishes at zero.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Conditional Knight identities give
\begin{align*}
 Z_{ij}(f)
 &=\int_0^{h_f(x_{ij})}
   \mathbb P(U_{ij}\le u\mid x_{ij})\,du-\tau h_f(x_{ij}),\\
 \widetilde Z_{ij}(f)
 &=\int_0^{h_f(x_{ij})}
   \mathbb P(U_{ij}\le u\mid\delta_i,x_{ij})\,du-\tau h_f(x_{ij}).
\end{align*}
The linear terms therefore cancel exactly, proving
\eqref{app:eq:rij-exact}.  Since $|D_i(x,u)|\le1$, the Lipschitz assertion
follows.
\end{proof}

For a block $I$, define the weighted centered process
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb G_I(f)=
 \sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}w_{ij}
 \{\overline Z_{ij}(f)-\ell_{ij,f}\}.                           \label{app:eq:block-process}
\end{equation}
The identity
\begin{align}
 \overline Z_{ij}(f)-\ell_{ij,f}
 &=\{\overline Z_{ij}(f)-Z_{ij}(f)\}
   +\{Z_{ij}(f)-\widetilde Z_{ij}(f)\}
   +\{\widetilde Z_{ij}(f)-\ell_{ij,f}\}                       \label{app:eq:three-way-decomposition}
\end{align}
is used below.

\begin{lemma}[Pointwise symmetrization terms]
\label{app:lem:pointwise-symm}
Let $I$ be a coupled block and let $\varepsilon_{ij}$ be independent
Rademacher variables, independent of the coupled data.  For every centered
class $\mathcal H$,
\begin{align}
 &\mathbb E\sup_{h_f\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\sum_{i\in I}\sum_jw_{ij}
 \{\widetilde Z_{ij}(f)-\ell_{ij,f}\}\right|
 \le 4\mathcal R_I(\mathcal H),                                \label{app:eq:error-symm}\\
 &\mathbb E\sup_{h_f\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\sum_{i\in I}\sum_jw_{ij}
 \{\overline Z_{ij}(f)-Z_{ij}(f)\}\right|
 \le 4\mathcal R_I(\mathcal H),                                \label{app:eq:design-symm}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_I(\mathcal H)=
 \mathbb E\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}w_{ij}\varepsilon_{ij}h(x_{ij}^*)\right|.
                                                                    \label{app:eq:RI}
\end{equation}
Furthermore, with
\begin{equation}
 R_i(f)=\mathbb E\{r_{ij}(f)\mid\delta_i\},                    \label{app:eq:Ri}
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E\sup_{h_f\in\mathcal H}
 \left|\sum_{i\in I}\sum_jw_{ij}\{r_{ij}(f)-R_i(f)\}\right|
 \le4\mathcal R_I(\mathcal H).                                 \label{app:eq:r-centered-symm}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \eqref{app:eq:error-symm}, condition on $(X_i^*,\delta_i^*)_{i\in I}$.
The measurement errors are independent across all $(i,j)$ in the coupled
block by Assumption~\ref{Assumption 1.}\ref{ass_1_2}.  Conditional
symmetrization gives a factor two, and the check-loss difference is
one-Lipschitz in $h_f(x_{ij}^*)$ and equals zero for $h_f=0$.  The scalar
contraction inequality gives the second factor two.

For \eqref{app:eq:design-symm}, $Z_{ij}(f)$ is a deterministic one-Lipschitz
function of $h_f(x_{ij})$.  The design points are independent within a coupled
block, so ordinary symmetrization and contraction apply.  Finally, conditional
on $\delta_i^*$, the variables $x_{ij}^*$ are independent and the map in
Lemma~\ref{app:lem:exact-spatial} is one-Lipschitz.  The same argument proves
\eqref{app:eq:r-centered-symm}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Uniform control of the blockwise spatial mean}

For a block $I$, set
\begin{equation}
 \Pi_I(x,u)=\frac1{|I|}\sum_{i\in I}D_i(x,u).                   \label{app:eq:PiI}
\end{equation}
By the tower property, $\mathbb E D_i(x,u)=0$ for every $(x,u)$.

\begin{lemma}[Grid bound for $\Pi_I$]
\label{app:lem:grid-Pi}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{assump:lip} and \ref{ass:tail_multi_d} hold.  There is
a constant $C_\Pi$ depending only on the uniform Lipschitz constant such that,
for every coupled block $I$ and every $z\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\left[
 \sup_{x\in[0,1]^d,\ |u|\le T_n}|\Pi_I(x,u)|
 >C_\Pi\sqrt{\frac{(s+1)\log\{n(1+T_n)\}+z}{|I|}}
 \right]\le2e^{-z}.                                           \label{app:eq:Pi-tail}
\end{equation}
Consequently, simultaneously over all $2L$ blocks,
\begin{equation}
 \max_I\sup_{x,|u|\le T_n}|\Pi_I(x,u)|
 =O_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt{\Gamma_n}),
 \qquad
 \Gamma_n=\frac{(s+1)L\log\{n(1+T_n)\}}{n}.                  \label{app:eq:Gamma-derived}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Only the coordinates $x_S$ matter.  Cover $[0,1]^s\times[-T_n,T_n]$ by a
rectangular grid with mesh $q^{-1}$, where $q=\lceil n(1+T_n)\rceil$.  Its cardinality is at most $Cq^s\{q(1+T_n)\}$.  At each grid point, the
summands $D_i$ are independent, centered, and bounded by one, so Hoeffding's
inequality and a union bound give
\[
 \max_{\text{grid}}|\Pi_I|
 \lesssim\sqrt{\frac{(s+1)\log\{n(1+T_n)\}+z}{|I|}}
\]
with probability at least $1-2e^{-z}$.  The Lipschitz condition adds at most
$C/q$, which is smaller than the displayed stochastic term.  Since
$|I|\asymp n/L$, a union bound over the $2L$ blocks proves
\eqref{app:eq:Gamma-derived}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Spatial-mean contribution]
\label{app:lem:spatial-mean}
On the event in \eqref{app:eq:Gamma-derived}, for every $t>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{f\in\mathcal F_\Delta(t)}
 \left|\sum_I\sum_{i\in I}\sum_jw_{ij}R_i(f)\right|
 \le Cc_0^{-1/2}b_Bt\sqrt{\Gamma_n}.                            \label{app:eq:spatial-mean-bound}
\end{equation}
Hence, for every $\eta>0$,
\begin{equation}
 Cc_0^{-1/2}b_Bt\sqrt{\Gamma_n}
 \le \eta t^2+C_\eta b_B^2\Gamma_n.                            \label{app:eq:young-spatial}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Because $T_n=2B$ and $|h_f|\le2B$, Fubini's theorem and
\eqref{app:eq:PiI} give
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{i\in I}\sum_jw_{ij}R_i(f)
 &=\frac{|I|}{n}\int_{[0,1]^d}\int_0^{h_f(x)}\Pi_I(x,u)\,du\,dQ(x).
\end{align*}
Its absolute value is at most
\[
 \frac{|I|}{n}\sup_{x,u}|\Pi_I(x,u)|\,\|h_f\|_1
 \le\frac{|I|}{n}\sup_{x,u}|\Pi_I(x,u)|\,\|h_f\|_2.
\]
The blocks partition $[n]$, and Lemma~\ref{app:lem:norm-comparison} gives
$\|h_f\|_2\le c_0^{-1/2}b_Bt$.  Summing over the blocks proves
\eqref{app:eq:spatial-mean-bound}; Young's inequality gives
\eqref{app:eq:young-spatial}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Localized empirical-process inequality}

\begin{proposition}[Localized process bound]
\label{app:prop:localized-process}
There are constants $C_1,C_2<\infty$ such that, for every $t>0$,
\begin{align}
 &\mathbb E\left[
 \sup_{f\in\mathcal F_\Delta(t)}
 |\overline Z(f)-\widehat Z(f)|\,\mathbbm 1_{\Omega_\beta}
 \right]\nonumber\\
 &\qquad\le
 C_1\sum_{\ell=1}^L\sum_{a\in\{e,o\}}
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}\{\mathcal H_2(c_0^{-1/2}b_Bt)\}
 +C_2c_0^{-1/2}b_Bt\sqrt{\Gamma_n}.                            \label{app:eq:localized-process}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{app:eq:three-way-decomposition} block by block.  The measurement
error term and the design term are bounded by
\eqref{app:eq:error-symm} and \eqref{app:eq:design-symm}.  For the middle term,
use Lemma~\ref{app:lem:exact-spatial}, split $r_{ij}(f)$ into
$r_{ij}(f)-R_i(f)$ and $R_i(f)$, apply
\eqref{app:eq:r-centered-symm} to the first part and
Lemma~\ref{app:lem:spatial-mean} to the second part.  Finally use the local
inclusion \eqref{app:eq:local-inclusion}.  All suprema are nonnegative because
the centered class contains zero, so the coupling indicator can be removed
after passing to the coupled block.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Convex localization and completion of the proof}

\begin{lemma}[Convex boundary reduction]
\label{app:lem:convex-boundary}
Suppose $\mathcal F$ is convex, $f^*\in\mathcal F$, and $\widehat f$ minimizes
\eqref{main_est}.  If $c_0\Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)>t^2$, there exists
$f_t\in\mathcal F_\Delta(t)$ satisfying
$c_0\Delta^2(f_t-f^*)=t^2$ and $\widehat Z(f_t)\le0$.  Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \{c_0\Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)>t^2\}
 \subseteq
 \left\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal F_\Delta(t)}
 |\overline Z(f)-\widehat Z(f)|\ge t^2\right\}.                \label{app:eq:boundary-inclusion}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The map
$\mu\mapsto c_0\Delta^2\{\mu(\widehat f-f^*)\}$ is continuous, vanishes at
zero, and exceeds $t^2$ at one.  Choose $\mu_t\in(0,1)$ at the first crossing
and let $f_t=f^*+\mu_t(\widehat f-f^*)$.  Convexity of $\mathcal F$ gives
$f_t\in\mathcal F$, while convexity of the empirical check loss gives
\[
 \widehat Z(f_t)\le(1-\mu_t)\widehat Z(f^*)+\mu_t\widehat Z(\widehat f)\le0.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{app:lem:curvature},
$\overline Z(f_t)\ge C_0\Delta^2(f_t-f^*)\ge t^2$.  Therefore
$|\overline Z(f_t)-\widehat Z(f_t)|\ge t^2$, proving
\eqref{app:eq:boundary-inclusion}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Critical-radius implication]
\label{app:lem:critical-radius}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \Psi_n(r)=C_*\left[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^L\sum_{a\in\{e,o\}}
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}\{\mathcal H_2(c_0^{-1/2}b_Br)\}
 +b_B^2\Gamma_n\right],                                       \label{app:eq:Psi-star}
\end{equation}
where $C_*$ is a sufficiently large universal constant, and let
$r_n=\inf\{r>0:\Psi_n(r)\le r^2/64\}$.  If a sub-root majorant of the first
term is used, then for every $M\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\{c_0\Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)>M^2r_n^2\}
 \le \frac{C}{M}+\mathbb P(\Omega_\beta^c)+o(1).               \label{app:eq:Op-tail}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{app:lem:convex-boundary} with $t=Mr_n$, split according to
$\Omega_\beta$, and use Markov's inequality together with
Proposition~\ref{app:prop:localized-process}.  Apply
\eqref{app:eq:young-spatial} with a sufficiently small $\eta$ and absorb the
resulting multiple of $t^2$ into the left-hand side.  The sub-root property
gives
$\Psi_n(Mr_n)\le M\Psi_n(r_n)\le Mr_n^2/64$, whereas the threshold is
$M^2r_n^2$.  This yields the $C/M$ term.  The grid event from
Lemma~\ref{app:lem:grid-Pi} has probability tending to one, and the coupling
error is handled separately.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}]
Lemma~\ref{app:lem:critical-radius} implies
$\Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)=O_{\mathbb P}(r_n^2)$.  The norm comparison
\eqref{app:eq:norm-comparison} then gives
\[
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2=O_{\mathbb P}(b_B^2r_n^2).
\]
The explicit coupling term follows from
\eqref{app:eq:total-coupling-error}.  This proves
\eqref{eq:general_convex_rate_full}.
\end{proof}


\section{Trend Filtering}
\label{app:trend-filtering}

\subsection{Localized entropy calculation}

For a block $I$, let $M_I=\sum_{i\in I}m_i$ and define
\[
 \|g\|_{I}^2=M_I^{-1}\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}g(x_{ij})^2.
\]
By \eqref{app:eq:balanced-weights}, the full-objective block process is bounded
by a constant times $M_I/N$ multiplied by the usual within-block empirical
Rademacher process.

\begin{lemma}[Trend-filtering block complexity]
\label{app:lem:tf-complexity}
Assume the entropy bound in Theorem~\ref{Theorem_2}.  Uniformly over the
interlaced blocks,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_I\{\mathcal H_2(r)\cap(\mathcal F_k(V)-f^*)\}
 \lesssim
 \frac{\sqrt{M_I}}{N}V^{1/(2k)}r^{1-1/(2k)}
 +\frac{B}{N}.                                                  \label{app:eq:tf-block-complexity}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_I\mathcal R_I\{\mathcal H_2(r)\cap(\mathcal F_k(V)-f^*)\}
 \lesssim
 V^{1/(2k)}r^{1-1/(2k)}\sqrt{\frac{L}{N}}
 +\frac{BL}{N}.                                                 \label{app:eq:tf-total-complexity}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Condition on the design.  Dudley's entropy integral, truncated below at a
constant multiple of $B/M_I$, yields
\begin{align*}
 &\mathbb E_\varepsilon\sup_{\substack{f\in\mathcal F_k(V)\\
 \|f-f^*\|_I\le Cr}}
 \left|\frac1{M_I}\sum_{i\in I}\sum_j\varepsilon_{ij}
 \{f(x_{ij})-f^*(x_{ij})\}\right|\\
 &\quad\lesssim \frac1{\sqrt{M_I}}
 \int_0^{Cr}\sqrt{V^{1/k}\epsilon^{-1/k}}\,d\epsilon
 +\frac{B}{M_I}\\
 &\quad\lesssim
 \frac{V^{1/(2k)}r^{1-1/(2k)}}{\sqrt{M_I}}+\frac{B}{M_I}.
\end{align*}
Multiplication by $M_I/N$ proves
\eqref{app:eq:tf-block-complexity}.  Since $M_I\asymp N/L$ and there are
$2L$ blocks, summing proves \eqref{app:eq:tf-total-complexity}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_2}]
Insert \eqref{app:eq:tf-total-complexity} into the critical-radius inequality.
Ignoring the lower-order term $BL/N$, the stochastic fixed point is
\[
 r^2\asymp V^{1/(2k)}r^{1-1/(2k)}\sqrt{L/N}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 r^2\asymp V^{2/(2k+1)}(L/N)^{2k/(2k+1)}.
\]
Adding $\Gamma_n$ and applying Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} proves
\eqref{tf_rate}.
\end{proof}


\section{High-dimensional Quantile Linear Regression}
\label{app:linear}

\begin{lemma}[Weighted linear-class complexity]
\label{app:lem:linear-complexity}
Let $I$ be an interlaced block and let $X_I^w$ be the weighted matrix from
\eqref{eq:An_linear_full}.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_I\{x\mapsto x^\top(\beta-\beta^*):
 \|\beta\|_1,\|\beta^*\|_1\le V\}
 \le \frac{2V\sqrt{2\log(2d)}}{n}
 \mathbb E\max_{q\in[d]}\|X^w_{I,\cdot q}\|_2.                \label{app:eq:linear-block}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $b=\beta-\beta^*$, $\|b\|_1\le2V$, and therefore
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R_I
 &\le \mathbb E\sup_{\|b\|_1\le2V}
 \left|\frac1n\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j=1}^{m_i}
 \frac{\varepsilon_{ij}}{m_i}x_{ij}^\top b\right|\\
 &=\frac{2V}{n}\mathbb E
 \left\|\sum_{i\in I}\sum_j\frac{\varepsilon_{ij}}{m_i}x_{ij}\right\|_\infty.
\end{align*}
Conditional on the design, the $q$th coordinate is sub-Gaussian with variance
proxy $\|X^w_{I,\cdot q}\|_2^2$.  The standard maximal inequality for $d$
sub-Gaussian variables proves \eqref{app:eq:linear-block}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm_hd}]
Sum \eqref{app:eq:linear-block} over the $2L$ blocks and insert the result into
Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}.  This gives \eqref{eq:hd_rate_full}.  If
$|x_{ij,q}|\le C_x$ and $m_i\asymp m$, then
\[
 \|X^w_{I,\cdot q}\|_2^2
 =\sum_{i\in I}\sum_j\frac{x_{ij,q}^2}{m_i^2}
 \lesssim\frac{|I|}{m}\asymp\frac{n}{Lm}.
\]
Thus $n^{-1}\mathcal A_n\lesssim\sqrt{L/(nm)}=\sqrt{L/N}$.
The theorem concerns prediction error.  A bound for $\|\widehat\beta-\beta^*\|$
would require a separate identifiability condition on the design.
\end{proof}


\section{Quantile \texorpdfstring{$K$}{K}-NN Fused Lasso}
\label{app:knn}

\subsection{A graph-complexity inequality}

Let $G=(V,E)$ be a connected graph on $N$ vertices, let $D_G$ be an oriented
incidence matrix, and let $D_G^\dagger$ be its Moore--Penrose inverse.  Define
\begin{equation}
 \rho(G)=\max_{e\in E}\|(D_G^\dagger)_{\cdot e}\|_2.            \label{app:eq:rhoG}
\end{equation}
This is a deterministic graph parameter.  The revised theorem states its
required bound explicitly rather than hiding it inside an unspecified
``polylogarithmic graph event.''

\begin{proposition}[Weighted graph-TV complexity]
\label{app:prop:graph-complexity}
Suppose \eqref{app:eq:balanced-weights} holds and every interlaced block contains
$M_I\asymp N/L$ vertices.  For
\[
 \mathcal U(r,V)=\{u\in\mathbb R^N:\|u\|_{n,m}\le r,
 \ \|D_Gu\|_1\le2V\},
\]
we have
\begin{equation}
 \sum_I\mathcal R_I\{\mathcal U(r,V)\}
 \lesssim r\sqrt{\frac{L}{N}}
 +\frac{LV\rho(G)\sqrt{\log(2|E|)}}{N}.                         \label{app:eq:graph-complexity}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a block $I$, let $a_I\in\mathbb R^N$ have coordinates
$(a_I)_v=w_v\varepsilon_v\mathbbm 1(v\in I)$.  Since $G$ is connected,
\[
 u=\bar u\mathbf 1_N+D_G^\dagger D_Gu,
 \qquad \bar u=N^{-1}\mathbf 1_N^\top u.
\]
Hence
\begin{align}
 |a_I^\top u|
 &\le |\bar u|\,|\mathbf 1_N^\top a_I|
   +\|(D_G^\dagger)^\top a_I\|_\infty\|D_Gu\|_1.             \label{app:eq:graph-decomp}
\end{align}
The balance condition gives $\|u\|_2\lesssim\sqrt N r$, so
$|\bar u|\lesssim r$.  Also,
\[
 \mathbb E|\mathbf 1_N^\top a_I|
 \le\left(\sum_{v\in I}w_v^2\right)^{1/2}
 \lesssim (NL)^{-1/2}.
\]
For each edge $e$, the random variable
$\{(D_G^\dagger)^\top a_I\}_e$ is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy at most
$C\rho(G)^2/N^2$.  Therefore
\[
 \mathbb E\|(D_G^\dagger)^\top a_I\|_\infty
 \lesssim\frac{\rho(G)\sqrt{\log(2|E|)}}{N}.
\]
Taking the supremum in \eqref{app:eq:graph-decomp} and summing over the $2L$
blocks proves \eqref{app:eq:graph-complexity}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Graph geometry and target total variation}

The following deterministic conditions are the graph events invoked in
Assumption~\ref{ass_knn}.  They are stated explicitly so that every rate in the
theorem is tied to a checkable quantity:
\begin{align}
 &G_K\text{ is connected and }\rho(G_K)\le\rho_N,              \label{app:eq:graph-event-rho}\\
 &\sup_{x\in[0,1]^d}R_K(x)\le C_R(K/N)^{1/d},                  \label{app:eq:knn-radius}\\
 &Q\{x:x_v\in N_K(x)\}\le C_{\mathrm O}K/N\quad\text{for every vertex }v. \label{app:eq:overlap}
\end{align}
Here $R_K(x)$ is the distance from $x$ to its $K$th nearest sample point.

\begin{lemma}[Graph-TV scale for a piecewise-Lipschitz target]
\label{app:lem:graph-tv-scale}
Suppose the maximum degree of $G_K$ is at most $C_DK$, the radius event
\eqref{app:eq:knn-radius} holds, the empirical fraction of vertices in the $r_N$-neighborhood of the singularity set is at most $C_{\mathcal S}r_N$, and $f^*$ is bounded and piecewise Lipschitz
with a discontinuity set whose $r$-neighborhood has $Q$-measure at most $C_Sr$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \|D_{G_K}\theta^*\|_1
 \lesssim N^{1-1/d}K^{1+1/d},
 \qquad \theta_v^*=f^*(x_v).                                  \label{app:eq:TV-scale-proved}
\end{equation}
In particular, if $K$ is polylogarithmic in $N$, this is
$N^{1-1/d}\operatorname{polylog}(N)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $r_N=C_R(K/N)^{1/d}$.  Vertices outside the $r_N$-neighborhood of the
singularity set have all graph neighbors in the same Lipschitz region, so each
incident edge contributes at most $C r_N$.  There are at most $C NK$ such
edges, giving $C NKr_N$.  The number of vertices in the boundary tube is at
most $C Nr_N$ on the design regularity event; each has degree at most $C_DK$
and each edge difference is at most $2B$.  This contributes the same order
$CNKr_N$.  Substituting $r_N=(K/N)^{1/d}$ proves
\eqref{app:eq:TV-scale-proved}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Off-sample transfer]
\label{app:lem:knn-transfer}
On the events \eqref{app:eq:knn-radius}--\eqref{app:eq:overlap}, for every
$u\in\mathbb R^N$,
\begin{equation}
 \int\left\{\frac1K\sum_{x_v\in N_K(x)}u_v\right\}^2dQ(x)
 \le C\|u\|_{n,m}^2.                                          \label{app:eq:estimation-transfer}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for a bounded piecewise-Lipschitz $f^*$,
\begin{equation}
 \int\left\{f^*(x)-\frac1K\sum_{x_v\in N_K(x)}f^*(x_v)\right\}^2dQ(x)
 \lesssim (K/N)^{1/d}.                                        \label{app:eq:approx-transfer}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Jensen's inequality and \eqref{app:eq:overlap} give
\begin{align*}
 \int\left\{K^{-1}\sum_{v\in N_K(x)}u_v\right\}^2dQ(x)
 &\le K^{-1}\sum_vu_v^2Q\{x:v\in N_K(x)\}\\
 &\le C N^{-1}\sum_vu_v^2\lesssim\|u\|_{n,m}^2.
\end{align*}
For the approximation term, set $r_N=C_R(K/N)^{1/d}$.  Outside the
$r_N$-tube around the discontinuity set, all neighbors lie in the same
Lipschitz component and the squared error is $O(r_N^2)$.  Inside the tube, the
squared error is at most $4B^2$, while the tube has probability $O(r_N)$.
Therefore the integrated error is $O(r_N^2+r_N)=O(r_N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_5}]
For $u=\theta-\theta^*$, feasibility gives
$\|D_{G_K}u\|_1\le2V_n$.  Proposition~\ref{app:prop:graph-complexity} and the
critical-radius theorem yield
\begin{equation}
 \|\widehat\theta-\theta^*\|_{n,m}^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\left[b_B^2\left\{
 \frac{L}{N}+\frac{LV_n\rho_N\sqrt{\log(2|E_K|)}}{N}
 +\Gamma_n\right\}\right].                                   \label{app:eq:knn-insample-final}
\end{equation}
Apply Lemma~\ref{app:lem:knn-transfer} to
$u=\widehat\theta-\theta^*$ and add the approximation bound
\eqref{app:eq:approx-transfer}.  This proves the off-sample statement in the
revised Theorem~\ref{Theorem_5}.  Finally,
Lemma~\ref{app:lem:graph-tv-scale} gives the displayed specialization when
$V_n\asymp N^{1-1/d}K^{1+1/d}$ and $\rho_N$ is polylogarithmic.
\end{proof}


\section{For \texorpdfstring{$f^*\notin\mathcal F$}{f* not in F} and a Nonconvex Class}
\label{app:nonconvex}

Let $f^\circ\in\mathcal F$ be the near-best approximant in
\eqref{eq:nonconvex_projection_full}, write $h^\circ=f^\circ-f^*$, and suppose
$\phi_n=\|h^\circ\|_\infty\le R$.  Define the centered star hull
\[
 \mathcal H^\star=\{\mu(f-f^*):f\in\mathcal F,\ \mu\in[0,1]\}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Approximation-adjusted localization]
\label{app:lem:nonconvex-localization}
Let $\widehat f$ minimize \eqref{main_est_nonconvex}.  There is a constant
$C_A$ depending only on the curvature constants such that, if
\begin{equation}
 c_0\Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)>t^2+C_A\phi_n^2,                  \label{app:eq:nonconvex-large}
\end{equation}
then there is $\widetilde h\in\mathcal H^\star$ satisfying
$c_0\Delta^2(\widetilde h)=t^2+C_A\phi_n^2$ and
\begin{align}
 t^2
 &\le 2\sup_{\substack{h\in\mathcal H^\star\\
 c_0\Delta^2(h)\le t^2+C_A\phi_n^2}}
 |(\mathbb P-\mathbb P_n)\ell_h|.                              \label{app:eq:nonconvex-process-reduction}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose $\mu\in(0,1)$ so that
$\widetilde h=\mu(\widehat f-f^*)$ lies on the boundary in
\eqref{app:eq:nonconvex-large}.  Convexity of the check loss and optimality of
$\widehat f$ over $\mathcal F$ imply
\[
 \mathbb P_n\ell_{\widetilde h}
 \le\mu\mathbb P_n\ell_{\widehat f-f^*}
 \le\mu\mathbb P_n\ell_{h^\circ}.
\]
By curvature,
$\mathbb P\ell_{\widetilde h}\ge C_0\Delta^2(\widetilde h)$, while
\eqref{app:eq:upper-curvature} gives
$\mathbb P\ell_{h^\circ}\le(\overline f/2)\phi_n^2$.  Add and subtract the
population losses of $\widetilde h$ and $h^\circ$.  Since both
$\widetilde h$ and $h^\circ$ belong to the displayed localized star hull once
$C_A$ is large enough, the two stochastic differences are bounded by the same
absolute empirical-process supremum.  Choosing $C_A$ to absorb the deterministic
approximation terms proves \eqref{app:eq:nonconvex-process-reduction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_non_convex}]
Apply Proposition~\ref{app:prop:localized-process} to the localized centered
star hull.  The proof of that proposition uses only centering, conditional
independence, and Lipschitz contraction, so it remains valid without convexity
of $\mathcal F$.  Lemma~\ref{app:lem:nonconvex-localization} then replaces the
convex boundary lemma.  The critical-radius argument gives
\[
 \Delta^2(\widehat f-f^*)=O_{\mathbb P}(r_n^2+\phi_n^2).
\]
Finally use Lemma~\ref{app:lem:norm-comparison} to obtain
\eqref{eq:nonconvex_rate_full}.
\end{proof}


\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_nn_rate}}
\label{app:nn}

Set
\begin{equation}
 A_N=S_NL_N\log(2S_N).                                         \label{app:eq:AN-network}
\end{equation}
Assumption~\ref{assump_nn} supplies both an approximating network and the
uniform empirical entropy bound
\[
 \log N(\epsilon,\mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}},\|\cdot\|_{n,m})
 \lesssim A_N\log(CB/\epsilon).
\]

\begin{lemma}[Localized network complexity]
\label{app:lem:nn-complexity}
For $0<r\le B$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_I\mathcal R_I\{\mathcal H_2(r)\cap
 (\mathcal F_{\mathrm{NN}}-f^*)\}
 \lesssim
 r\sqrt{\frac{A_NL}{N}\log\frac{CB}{r}}
 +\frac{BA_NL\log N}{N}.                                      \label{app:eq:nn-complexity}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For a block with $M_I\asymp N/L$ observations, the full-objective process is
$M_I/N$ times the within-block normalized process.  Dudley's inequality and
the entropy assumption give
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R_I
 &\lesssim \frac{\sqrt{M_I}}{N}
 \int_0^{Cr}\sqrt{A_N\log(CB/\epsilon)}\,d\epsilon
 +\frac{BA_N\log N}{N}\\
 &\lesssim \frac{r}{\sqrt{NL}}
 \sqrt{A_N\log(CB/r)}+\frac{BA_N\log N}{N}.
\end{align*}
Summing over the $2L$ blocks proves \eqref{app:eq:nn-complexity}; constants are
absorbed into $\lesssim$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_nn_rate}]
Let
\[
 a_N^2=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal P}
 N^{-2p/(2p+K)}\operatorname{polylog}(N).
\]
Assumption~\ref{assump_nn} gives a near-best network with
$\phi_N^2\lesssim a_N^2$ and chooses the architecture so that
\begin{equation}
 \frac{A_NL\log N}{N}\lesssim a_N^2.                           \label{app:eq:nn-balance}
\end{equation}
The fixed point generated by \eqref{app:eq:nn-complexity} is therefore
$r_N^2\lesssim a_N^2+\Gamma_n$.  Apply
Theorem~\ref{Theorem_non_convex} to obtain
\[
 \|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2
 =O_{\mathbb P}\{b_B^2(a_N^2+\Gamma_n)\},
\]
which is \eqref{nn_rate}.  The logarithmic factor is the one explicitly
encoded in the approximation, entropy, and architecture-balance conditions;
no additional unverified exponent is used.
\end{proof}


\section{ADMM Derivations}
\label{app:admm}

\subsection{Weighted check-loss proximal map}

For $w>0$, $\varrho>0$, and $a,y\in\mathbb R$, consider
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{prox}(a)=\argmin_\theta
 \left\{w\rho_\tau(y-\theta)+\frac\varrho2(\theta-a)^2\right\}. \label{app:eq:prox-problem}
\end{equation}
The subgradient condition is
\[
 0\in \varrho(\theta-a)-w\{\tau-\mathbbm 1(y-\theta<0)\},
\]
with the usual interval-valued subgradient at $\theta=y$.  Solving the three
cases gives
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{prox}(a)=
 \begin{cases}
 a+w\tau/\varrho,&y>a+w\tau/\varrho,\\
 a-w(1-\tau)/\varrho,&y<a-w(1-\tau)/\varrho,\\
 y,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}                                                    \label{app:eq:prox-solution}
\end{equation}
Taking $w=w_{ij}$ yields the coordinate update in the main paper.

\subsection{Scaled dual update}

For a split problem $\min_{\theta,z}g(\theta)+h(z)$ subject to $\theta=z$, the
scaled augmented Lagrangian is
\[
 g(\theta)+h(z)+\frac\varrho2\|\theta-z+u\|_2^2
 -\frac\varrho2\|u\|_2^2.
\]
Alternating minimization in $\theta$ and $z$ is followed by
\begin{equation}
 u^{t+1}=u^t+\theta^{t+1}-z^{t+1}.                              \label{app:eq:scaled-dual}
\end{equation}
There is no maximization of $\|\theta+u-z\|_2^2$ over $u$; such a maximization
is unbounded.  Equations \eqref{app:eq:prox-solution} and
\eqref{app:eq:scaled-dual} establish the corrected Algorithms 1 and 2.


\section{Auxiliary Empirical-process Lemmas}
\label{app:auxiliary}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional symmetrization]
\label{app:lem:conditional-symm}
Let $Z_1,\ldots,Z_M$ be conditionally independent given a sigma-field
$\mathcal A$, and let $\mathcal G$ be a class containing the zero function.
Then
\[
 \mathbb E\left[\sup_{g\in\mathcal G}
 \left|\sum_{q=1}^M\{g(Z_q)-\mathbb E(g(Z_q)\mid\mathcal A)\}\right|
 \middle|\mathcal A\right]
 \le2\mathbb E\left[\sup_{g\in\mathcal G}
 \left|\sum_{q=1}^M\varepsilon_qg(Z_q)\right|
 \middle|\mathcal A\right].
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Introduce a conditionally independent ghost sample $Z_1',\ldots,Z_M'$, apply
Jensen's inequality, and use the conditional exchangeability of
$(Z_q,Z_q')$ to insert Rademacher signs.  The triangle inequality gives the
factor two.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Contraction]
\label{app:lem:contraction}
If $\phi_q(0)=0$ and every $\phi_q$ is one-Lipschitz, then for every centered
class $\mathcal V\subset\mathbb R^M$,
\[
 \mathbb E_\varepsilon\sup_{v\in\mathcal V}
 \left|\sum_{q=1}^M\varepsilon_q\phi_q(v_q)\right|
 \le2\mathbb E_\varepsilon\sup_{v\in\mathcal V}
 \left|\sum_{q=1}^M\varepsilon_qv_q\right|.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is the Ledoux--Talagrand contraction inequality in its absolute-value
form.  It is applied in this paper only after centering at $f^*$, which ensures
that the index set contains zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dudley integral with full-objective weighting]
\label{app:lem:dudley-weighted}
Let a block contain $M$ observations and let its total weight in the full
criterion be $\omega_I=\sum_{v\in I}w_v$.  Conditional on the design,
\[
 \mathbb E_\varepsilon\sup_{g\in\mathcal G}
 \left|\sum_{v\in I}w_v\varepsilon_vg_v\right|
 \lesssim \omega_I\inf_{\alpha>0}\left[
 \alpha+\frac1{\sqrt M}\int_\alpha^{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal G)}
 \sqrt{\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal G,\|\cdot\|_I)}\,d\epsilon
 \right].
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By balanced weights, the left-hand side is bounded by a constant times
$\omega_I$ multiplied by the normalized empirical Rademacher process on the
block.  Apply the usual chaining proof of Dudley's inequality to that
normalized process.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Sub-root scaling]
\label{app:lem:subroot}
If $\psi$ is nondecreasing and $r\mapsto\psi(r)/r$ is nonincreasing, then
$\psi(Mr)\le M\psi(r)$ for $M\ge1$.  Consequently, if
$\psi(r_*)\le r_*^2/c$, then
$\psi(Mr_*)/(M^2r_*^2)\le1/(cM)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first claim follows directly from monotonicity of $\psi(r)/r$; the second
is immediate.
\end{proof}


\section{Beta-mixing Coefficients and Coupling}
\label{beta_mix_intro}

For sigma-fields $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$, define
\begin{equation}
 \beta(\mathcal A,\mathcal B)
 =\frac12\sup\sum_{r,s}
 |\mathbb P(A_r\cap B_s)-\mathbb P(A_r)\mathbb P(B_s)|,         \label{app:eq:beta-fields}
\end{equation}
where the supremum is over finite measurable partitions
$\{A_r\}\subset\mathcal A$ and $\{B_s\}\subset\mathcal B$.  For a process
$\{W_i\}_{i\in\mathbb Z}$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_W(k)=\sup_t
 \beta\{\sigma(W_i:i\le t),\sigma(W_i:i\ge t+k)\}.            \label{app:eq:beta-process}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Berbee coupling]
\label{app:lem:berbee}
Let $Z_1,\ldots,Z_q$ be a finite subsequence whose consecutive elements are
separated by at least $k$ in the original beta-mixing process.  There are
independent variables $Z_1^*,\ldots,Z_q^*$ with the same respective marginal
laws such that
\[
 \mathbb P\{(Z_1,\ldots,Z_q)\ne(Z_1^*,\ldots,Z_q^*)\}
 \le(q-1)\beta_W(k).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Couple $Z_2$ to an independent copy conditional on $Z_1$, then couple $Z_3$
conditional on $(Z_1,Z_2)$, and continue.  At each step, the total-variation
coupling error is at most $\beta_W(k)$.  A union bound over the $q-1$ steps
proves the result.
\end{proof}

The proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1} applies this lemma to the joint cluster
$W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i)$.  This preserves the contemporaneous dependence among
the design, spatial effect, and measurement-error vector.  Applying the lemma
separately to the coordinate processes would not, in general, preserve their
joint law and is therefore not used.

\end{revision}

\clearpage

For transparency and to preserve the full structure of the supplied 133-page source, the original detailed derivations are reproduced below in gray. Red passages state the implemented replacements. The gray derivations are retained only as a line-by-line comparison and are superseded wherever they conflict with the operative corrected proofs.
\end{revision}
\begin{legacycomparison}
% \input{a1_quantile_temporal_spatial_appendix_v14}
\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}}
\implementedchange{Appendices~\ref{app_b}--\ref{beta_mix_intro} above are the operative corrected supplement. They use $w_{ij}=1/(nm_i)$, the joint cluster $W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i)$, centered local classes, and the critical radius in \eqref{eq:critical_radius}. The gray material below is retained solely for line-by-line comparison with the supplied source.}
\label{app_b}


\subsubsection{Corrected Proof Roadmap and Normalization}
\begin{revision}
Write $w_{ij}=1/(nm_i)$ and
\[
 \ell_{ij}(f)=\rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\}
 -\rho_\tau\{y_{ij}-f^*(x_{ij})\}.
\]
Knight's identity and Assumption~\ref{assumptionA} imply
\[
 \overline Z(f):=\mathbb E\sum_{i,j}w_{ij}\ell_{ij}(f)
 \ge C_0\Delta^2(f-f^*),\qquad
 C_0=\min\{\underline f/2,R\underline f/4\},
\]
where $\Delta^2(h)=\int\min\{|h|,h^2\}\,dQ$. Since
$\|f\|_\infty,\|f^*\|_\infty\le B$,
\[
 \|f-f^*\|_2^2\le (1\vee 2B)\Delta^2(f-f^*).
\]
For every interlaced block $I=\mathcal J_{a,\ell}$, Berbee's lemma is applied once to the joint random element $W_i=(X_i,\delta_i,E_i)$; separate couplings of $X_i$, $\delta_i$, and $E_i$ are not used. The centered weighted block process is
\[
 \mathbb E\sup_{h\in\mathcal H(r)}
 \left|\frac1n\sum_{i\in I}\frac1{m_i}
 \sum_{j=1}^{m_i}\varepsilon_{ij}h(x_{ij}^*)\right|,
 \qquad
 \mathcal H(r)=\{f-f^*:f\in\mathcal F,\ \|f-f^*\|_2\le r\}.
\]
The conditional spatial-effect remainder is controlled separately by the
block average of
\[
 D_i(x,u)=\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid x_{i1}=x)
 -\mathbb P(U_{i1}\le u\mid\delta_i,x_{i1}=x),
 \qquad U_{ij}=\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}.
\]
The low-dimensional Lipschitz/grid condition gives the term
$\Gamma_n=(s+1)L_n\log(nT_n)/n$.  Combining curvature, joint coupling,
conditional symmetrization, contraction, and the tail-absorption condition
leads to the sub-root inequality
\[
 \Psi_n(r)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_n}\sum_{a\in\{e,o\}}
 \mathcal R_{a,\ell}\{\mathcal H(c_0^{-1/2}b_Br)\}+\Gamma_n
 \le r^2/64.
\]
The critical radius $r_n$ is the smallest $r$ satisfying this inequality,
and the basic inequality yields
$\|\widehat f-f^*\|_2^2=O_{\mathbb P}(b_B^2r_n^2)$, plus the explicit
coupling probability $n\beta_W(L_n)$. This roadmap supersedes any later line
that normalizes a block by $\sum_{i\in I}m_i$ without multiplying by the
block's weight in the full empirical objective.
\end{revision}

\subsubsection{Definitions and Notation}
To complete our proofs for the theorems, we provide important definitions.
% Let $J$ as in Definition~\ref{definition2}. 
% \begin{definition} 
% The $r$-th order $TV$-ball of radius $V$ is defined as: 
% \label{def:J}
% \beq 
% \label{eq197}
% B_J(V) \coloneqq B_{TV}^{(r)}(V) = \set{f: TV^{(r)}(f) = J(f) \le V}. 
% % \equiv\ \set{f: J(f)\le V}
% \beq
% \end{definition}
% \begin{definition}
% The $r$-th order trend filtering operator norm ball of radius $V$ as 
% \beq 
% \label{def:ball of trend filtering}
% BV^{(r)}(V) = \set{\theta \in \R^n: \norm{D^{(x,r)}(\theta)}_1 \le V}.
% \beq    
% \end{definition}

% Let $y\in \R^n$ be a vector of independent random variables and for a given quantile level $\tau \in (0,1)$.    

\begin{definition}
We introduce the Huber-type \cite{huber1992robust} loss. 
Let $\Delta_{nm}^2$ be a mapping of functions to real numbers,
the Huber-type loss function is elucidated as
% \beq 
% \label{huber loss}
% \Delta^2(f, \{x_{ij}\}) = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i} \sum_{j=1}^{m_i} \min\{\abs{f(x_{ij})}, (f(x_{ij}))^2\},
% \beq 
\beq 
\label{huber loss}
\Delta_{nm}^2(f) \,=\, \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i} \sum_{j=1}^{m_i} \min\{\abs{f(x_{ij})}, (f(x_{ij}))^2\},
\beq 
which, modulo constants, corresponds to a Huber loss, as delineated in \cite{huber1992robust}. The Huber loss serves as a natural lower bound for the quantile population loss, which provides the primary motivation for focusing on it.
Let $P(x_{ij})$ denote the distribution of $x_{ij}$. The population Huber loss is then given by
\begin{align}
\label{huber_def}
\Delta^2(f) &\,=\,  \int \min\{\abs{f(t)}, \abs{f(t)}^2\}dP(t).
\end{align}
We derive the rate of convergence by using the Huber-type  loss and $L_2$ loss. 
\end{definition}



% \begin{definition}
% \label{definition213}
% We define the Rademacher width of $K$ as
% \bea 
% R_{nj}(F_k(V),\{x_{ij}\}) = \ept{\sup_{f \in \gF_k(V)}  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})\mid \{x_{ij}\}_{i=1}^{n}}.
% \bea    
% \end{definition}


% \begin{definition}
% \label{definition213}
% We define its Empirical Rademacher width as 
% \bea 
% R_{mn}(K) = \ept{\sup_{f \in K}  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})\mid \{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{ij}}}.
% \bea    
% \end{definition}


% \begin{definition}
% \label{def_rade}
%  we define its Rademacher width 
% \bea 
% R(K) = \ept{R_{mn}(K)} = \ept{\sup_{f \in K}  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})}. 
% \bea
% \end{definition}


\begin{definition}
\label{definition213} Let $K$ be a function class with Rademacher width well defined. 
We define its Empirical Rademacher width as 
\bea 
R_{mn}(K) \,=\, \Ept{\xi \mid \{x_{ij}\}}{\sup_{f \in K}  \frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^n m_i}\sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})}.
\bea    
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}
\label{def_brade}
 We define its Rademacher width as
\bea 
 R(K) \,=\, \Ept{x}{\Ept{\xi \mid \{x_{ij}\}}{\sup_{f \in K}  \frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^n m_i}\sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})}} = \Ept{x, \xi}{\sup_{f \in K}  \frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^n m_i}\sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \xi_{ij} f(x_{ij})}. 
\bea
\end{definition}





\begin{definition}
  We define $\hat Z_{ij}(f)$ and  the empirical loss function $\hat Z(f)$ as 
\beq 
\label{348}
\hat Z_{ij}(f) =& \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij})) \\
=& \rho_{\tau}(f^*(x_{ij}) + \delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(\delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}). 
\beq 
\beq 
\label{295}
\hat Z(f) &\,=\, \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \hat Z_{ij}(f)\\
&\,=\, \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\set{ \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))}\\
&\,=\,  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\set{\rho_{\tau}(f^*_{ij} + \delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij} - f^*(x_{ij}))}.   
\beq 


We also define $ Z_{ij}(f)$ and $Z(f)$ as 
\beq 
\label{eq215}
Z_{ij}(f) \,\coloneqq\, & \Ept{\delta, e \mid x_{ij}}{\rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))} \\
=& \Ept{\delta, e \mid x_{ij}}{\rho_{\tau}(f^*(x_{ij}) + \delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(\delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij})}. 
\beq 

% \beq 
% \label{def_zf}
% Z(f) & \coloneqq  \ept{\hat Z(f)|\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{ij}}} \\
% &= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \ept{\rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))|\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{ij}}} \\
% &=  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\ept{\rho_{\tau}(f^*_{ij} + \delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))|\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{ij}} }, 
% \beq 
% where we use $\{x_{ij}\}$ as a shorthand for $\{x_{ij}\}_{i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l},\; j \in [m_i]}$.

\beq 
\label{def_zf}
Z(f) & \,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{\delta, e \mid \{x_{ij}\}}{\hat Z(f)} \\
&\,=\, \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \Ept{\delta, e \mid \{x_{ij}\}}{\rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))} \\
&\,=\,  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\Ept{\delta, e \mid \{x_{ij}\}}{\rho_{\tau}(f^*_{ij} + \delta_i(x_{ij}) + e_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))}, 
\beq 

where we use $\{x_{ij}\}$ as a shorthand for $\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{ij}}$ and $\set{\delta_i}$ as $\set{\delta_i}_{i=1}^n$.


We then define $\tilde Z_{ij}(f)$ and $\tilde Z(f)$ as 
\bea 
\tilde Z_{ij}(f) \,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{e \mid \delta_i, x_{ij}}{ \hat Z_{ij}(f)}.
\bea 
\beq 
\label{def_tilde_zf}
\tilde Z(f) \,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{e \mid \set{\delta_i},\set{x_{ij}}}{\hat Z(f)}.
\beq 



We define the population version $\bar Z_{ij}(f)$ and the population loss function as
\bea 
\bar Z_{ij}(f) \,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{x, \delta, e}{ \hat Z_{ij}(f)}.
\bea 
\beq 
\label{305}
\bar Z(f) \,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{x, \delta, e}{\hat Z(f)}. 
\beq  
\end{definition}
 


Note that the $\delta_i(x_{i1}), \dots, \delta_i(x_{im_i})$ are not independent for $i \in \set{1,\dots, n}$. 
Similarly, due to the definitions of $\hat Z_{ij}(f)$, $Z_{ij}(f)$, $\hat Z(f)$, and $Z(f)$, the terms $\hat Z_{i1}(f), \dots, \hat Z_{im_i}(f)$ are also not independent.  Also note, by definition that $f^*(x_{ij}) \,=\, F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}^{-1}(\tau)$, we know $f^* \in \argmin Z(f)$, and $Z(f^*) \,=\, 0$.  
% and $f^*(x_{ij}) = Q_{\tau}(y_i | x_i)$. from \eqref{eq44},


\begin{definition}
\label{definition3}
The constrained quantile trend filtering estimator for temporal-spatial data defined in \eqref{eq172} will be
\label{def}
    \beq 
\hat f = \argmin_{f \in F_k(V)} \hat Z(f),
\beq  where $F_k(V)$ is defined in \eqref{def_tvspace_tf}. 
\end{definition}


\subsubsection{Proof Sketches of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}}
\implementedchange{The proof now works on the event obtained by Berbee coupling of the joint clusters. With interlaced spacing $2L$, \[
\mathbb P(\Omega_\beta^c)\le n\beta_W(2L),
\]
and this term is carried through the final probability inequality before the critical-radius argument is applied.}

% To proceed in the proof of Theorems~\ref{Theorem_1}, 
\step{Step 1}
To get the convergence rate of $\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*)$, we will control the quantity $\sP\left( c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2  \right)$, where $c_0$ satisfies \eqref{eq_c0_range}. 

We will first consider the temporal dependence. To control 
that, we would need to use the $\beta-$mixing concept that are constructed in Appendix~\ref{beta_mix_intro}.
We then get the setup of the indices for \( |\mathcal{J}_{e,l}|, |\mathcal{J}_{o,l}| \asymp n/L \) in \eqref{odd_even_indices}. We let $\gI \,\coloneqq\, \bigcup_{l=1}^{L}(\mathcal J_{e,l}\cup \mathcal J_{o,l})$. For each fixed $l \in [L]$, we construct copies $x_{ij}^{*}, e_{ij}^{*}, \delta_i^{*}$ for $i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l}$ (and similarly for $\mathcal{J}_{o,l}$) via the coupling in Appendix~\ref{beta_mix_intro}. These copies have the same marginal distribution as $x_{ij}, e_{ij}, \delta_i$, and are mutually independent within each block $\mathcal{J}_{e,l}$ (but not across different blocks). Next, we let
\[
X_i \,\coloneqq\, (x_{i1},\dots,x_{im_i}),\qquad
E_i \,\coloneqq\, (e_{i1},\dots,e_{im_i}),\qquad
\Delta_i \,\coloneqq\, (\delta_{i1},\dots,\delta_{im_i}),
\]
and their coupled copies
\[
X_i^{*} \,\coloneqq\, (x_{i1}^{*},\dots,x_{im_i}^{*}),\qquad 
E_i^{*} \,\coloneqq\, (e_{i1}^{*},\dots,e_{im_i}^{*}),\qquad
\Delta_i^{*} \,\coloneqq\, (\delta_{i1}^{*},\dots,\delta_{im_i}^{*}),
\]
and then we define 
\beq 
\label{omega_definition}
&\Omega_x \,:=\, \left\{ X_i = X_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\},\\
&\Omega_e \,:=\, \left\{ E_i = E_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\}, \\
&\Omega_\delta \,:=\, \left\{ \Delta_i = \Delta_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\}, \\
&\Omega_\beta \,:=\, \Omega_x\cap \Omega_e\cap \Omega_\delta. 
\beq 

% $\Omega_x := \left\{ X_i = X_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\},$
% $\Omega_e := \left\{ E_i = E_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\}, \\ $
% $\Omega_\delta := \left\{ \delta_i = \delta_i^* \ \text{for all } i\in \gI \right\},$ and 
% $\Omega_\beta := \Omega_x\cap \Omega_e\cap \Omega_\delta .$ 
% \(\Omega_{x} = \{i: x_{ij} = x_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\), \(\Omega_{e} = \{i: e_{ij} = e_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\) and \(\Omega_{\delta} = \{i: \delta_{i} = \delta_{i}^{*}, \forall j \in [m_i]\}\), let \(\Omega_{\beta} = \Omega_{x} \cap \Omega_{e} \cap \Omega_{\delta} \).
Thus, we get 
\bea  
\sP\left( c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2 \right) \,\leq\, \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta}  \right) + \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta}^{c}\right). 
\bea 

\step{Step 2}
Then in Lemma~\ref{lemma_beta_decomp}, we show $\sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta}^{c}\right)$ is $O(\frac{1}{n})$.


\step{Step 3} 
It remains to bound the joint probability
$\sP\!\left(\{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f-f^*)>t^2\}\cap \Omega_\beta\right)$.
On the coupling event $\Omega_\beta$ (see Remark~\ref{remark:joint}),
the blockwise coupling reduces the original $\beta$-mixing sequence to an
independent-block structure. Consequently, the arguments in
Lemmas~\ref{lemma12}--\ref{lem_huber_rate} (based on symmetrization and
Rademacher complexities) yield an upper bound for
$\sP\!\left(\{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f-f^*)>t^2\}\cap \Omega_\beta\right)$
in terms of the corresponding blockwise Rademacher width terms. 
% The details of the proof are provided as follows. 
% We first define empirical loss function $\hat Z(f)$ as in \eqref{295}, 
% \bea 
% \hat Z(f) &= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}} \hat Z_{ij}(f)\\
% &= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{m_i}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\set{ \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))}.
% \bea and its conditional expectation parts as 
% \bea 
% Z(f) =  \ept{\hat Z(f)|\set{x_{ij}}}, \quad \tilde Z(f) \coloneqq  \ept{\hat Z(f)|\set{\delta_i},\set{x_{ij}}}, \quad \bar Z(f) \coloneqq  \ept{\hat Z(f)}. 
% \bea 
\step{Step 4} 
In details, with the above two steps, under Assumptions~\ref{Assumption 1.}, \ref{assumptionA}, \ref{assump:lip}, and \ref{ass:tail_multi_d}, the following results hold for any $t > 0$, 
\bea
&\sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2}  
\\ & \,\leq\, \frac{c_5}{t^2}\sum_{l=1}^L\Big(R_{\mathcal{J}_{e,l}}(\gF \cap \set{c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le  t^2})+ R_{\mathcal{J}_{o,l}}(\gF \cap \set{c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le  t^2})\Big) \,+\, \frac{c_4}{t^2}\frac{s\log n\log T_n}{n}, 
\bea  where $s = |S|$ is the intrinsic dimension from Assumption~\ref{assump:lip} (the number of active coordinates of $\Pi$), $T_n$ is from Assumption~\ref{ass:tail_multi_d}. Constant $c_{4}$ depends on $\tilde c_0$ in Assumption~\ref{ass:tail_multi_d}, $C_{\beta}$ in Assumption~\ref{ass_c_beta}, and $c_1$, $c_2$ in \eqref{beta_constants}, and $c_5$ depends on constants $C$ and $c$ in Assumption~\ref{asm:m}.

\step{Step 5}
By using \cref{lem_17},  we start by writing
\bea 
&R_{\mathcal{J}_{e,l}}\left(\gF\cap \set{f: c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*) \leq  t^2}\right) \,\leq\, R_{\mathcal{J}_{e,l}}\left(\gF\cap \set{f:\norm{f-f^*} \leq  c_0^{-1/2}\cdot b(B)\cdot t}\right), \,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\, \\
&R_{\mathcal{J}_{o,l}}\left(\gF\cap \set{f: c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*) \leq  t^2}\right) \,\leq\, R_{\mathcal{J}_{o,l}}\left(\gF\cap \set{f:\norm{f-f^*} \leq  c_0^{-1/2}\cdot b(B)\cdot t}\right),
\bea 
where $b(B) \,=\, \max\set{B^{1/2},1}$ is a constant that depends on $B$, with $B$ introduced in \eqref{F_2}. 

Let 
\bea
r_n^2 &\,\asymp\, \sum_{l=1}^L\Big(R_{\mathcal{J}_{e,l}}\left(\gF\cap B_2\left(c_0^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot b(B)\cdot r_n\right)\right) \\
&\qquad + R_{\mathcal{J}_{o,l}}\left(\gF\cap B_2\left(c_0^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot b(B)\cdot r_n\right)\right)\Big) \,+\, \frac{s\cdot \text{poly}(\log n)\log T_n}{n}.
\bea

Then we get the convergence rate of 
\beq
\label{eq828}
\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*)  \,=\, O_p \left(r_n^2\right).
\beq
This follows from Lemma~\ref{lem_huber_rate} and Corollary~\ref{cor_op}. 
 
% Then we have 
% \bea 
% \Delta^2(\hat{f} - f^*) &= O_p \left(\sum_{l=1}^L\left(R_{\mathcal{J}_{e,l}}\left(\mathcal{F}\cap \left\{f: \norm{f-f^*}_2\leq c_0^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot b(B)\cdot t\right\}\right)\right.\right. \\
% &\quad \left.\left. +R_{\mathcal{J}_{o,l}}\left(\mathcal{F}\cap \left\{f: \norm{f-f^*}_2\leq c_0^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot b(B)\cdot t\right\}\right) + \frac{s\cdot\text{poly}(\log n)\log (T_n)}{n}\right)\right).
% \bea  

The details of the proofs of the above steps are provided as follows. 

\subsubsection{Technical Results for Proofs of Theorem~\ref{Theorem_1}}

\subsubsection{Proof of Step 1 and 2}
\implementedchange{The separate coordinate copies have been replaced by one copy $W_i^*=(X_i^*,\delta_i^*,E_i^*)$ for each time cluster. Within each interlaced block the $W_i^*$ are independent, have the correct joint marginal law, and preserve $y_{ij}=f^*(x_{ij})+\delta_i(x_{ij})+e_{ij}$.}
To get the convergence rate of $\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*)$, we will control the quantity $\sP\left( c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2  \right)$, where $c_0$ satisfies \eqref{eq_c0_range}, we will first consider the temporal dependence. To control 
that, we would need to use the $\beta-$mixing concept. In the following analysis, we will then deal with this dependence.  

Specifically, we rely on the condition that \( cm \,\leq\, m_i \,\leq\, Cm \) for some positive constants \( c, C \). Additionally, we define
\begin{equation}
\label{equation}
L = \left\lceil \frac{1}{C_{\beta}} 2\log(n) \right\rceil,
\end{equation}
leading us to the conclusion that
\begin{equation}
\label{beta_decay_rate}
\beta_{x}(L) \,\leq\, \frac{1}{n^{2}},
\end{equation} where $\beta_{x}$ is defined in Assumption~\ref{ass_c_beta}.
This bound is equivalently satisfied by \( \beta_{\delta}(L) \) and \( \beta_{e}(L) \). Subsequently, throughout our discussion, we make frequent references to the copies of the random variables \(x_{ij}\), \( e_{ij} \), and \( \delta_i \) as they are constructed in Appendix~\ref{beta_mix_intro}. These copies are denoted as \( x_{ij}^{*} \), \( e_{ij}^{*} \), and \( \delta_{i}^{*} \), respectively. Furthermore, as delineated in Appendix~\ref{beta_mix_intro}, during the formation of these copies the quantities \( |\mathcal{J}_{e,l}| \) and \( |\mathcal{J}_{o,l}| \) in \eqref{odd_even_indices}, which represent the count of even and odd blocks respectively, satisfy \( |\mathcal{J}_{e,l}|, |\mathcal{J}_{o,l}| \asymp n/L \). Thus, there exist positive constants \( c_1 \) and \( c_2 \) such that
 \begin{equation}
 \label{beta_constants}
c_1 n/L \,\leq\, |\mathcal{J}_{e,l}|, |\mathcal{J}_{o,l}| \,\leq\, c_2 n/L, 
\quad L = \left\lceil \frac{1}{C_{\beta}} 2\log(n) \right\rceil.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_beta_decomp}  
Let $\hat f$, $f^*$ belong to $\mathcal{F}$, and let $\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*)$ be the Huber loss. Suppose Assumption~\ref{Assumption 1.} holds with the $\beta$-mixing condition satisfied in \eqref{beta_decay_rate} and the block indices $\mathcal{J}_{e,l}, \mathcal{J}_{o,l}$ satisfying $|\mathcal{J}_{e,l}|, |\mathcal{J}_{o,l}| \asymp n/L$ as in \eqref{odd_even_indices}. For each fixed $l \in [L]$, we construct identically distributed copies $\{x_{ij}^{*}, e_{ij}^{*}, \delta_i^{*}\}_{i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l}}$ that are mutually independent within the block $\mathcal{J}_{e,l}$ (and similarly for $\mathcal{J}_{o,l}$), via the coupling in Appendix~\ref{beta_mix_intro}. Let
$\Omega_{x}, \Omega_{e}, \Omega_{\delta}, \Omega_{\beta}$ be defined in \eqref{omega_definition}. 
% \(\Omega_{x} = \{i: x_{ij} = x_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\), \(\Omega_{e} = \{i: e_{ij} = e_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\) and \(\Omega_{\delta} = \{i: \delta_{i} = \delta_{i}^{*}\}\), let \(\Omega_{\beta} = \Omega_{x} \cap \Omega_{e} \cap \Omega_{\delta} \).
Then we have 
\bea  
\sP\left( c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2 \right) \,\leq\,  \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta} \right)  \,+\, \frac{\epsilon}{2}.
\bea
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Using a union bound argument and invoking Lemmas \ref{Lemma_9}, \ref{Lemma 10.}, and \ref{Lemma 11.}, we note that the following sets, 
    $\Omega_{x}, \Omega_{e}, \Omega_{\delta}$ in \eqref{omega_definition},
    % \(\Omega_{x} = \{i: x_{ij} = x_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\), \(\Omega_{e} = \{i: e_{ij} = e_{ij}^{*} \ \forall j \in [m_i]\}\) and \(\Omega_{\delta} = \{i: \delta_{i} = \delta_{i}^{*}\}\),
    satisfy the conditions
\[
\sP(\Omega_{x}^{c}) \,\leq\, n\beta_{x}(L) \,\leq\, \frac{1}{n}, \quad \sP(\Omega_{e}^{c}) \,\leq\, n\beta_{e}(L) \,\leq\, \frac{1}{n}, \quad \,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\, \quad \sP(\Omega_{\delta}^{c}) \,\leq\, n\beta_{\delta}(L) \,\leq\, \frac{1}{n}.
\]
Therefore letting 
\beq 
\label{event_beta}
\Omega_{\beta} \,=\, \Omega_{x} \cap \Omega_{e} \cap \Omega_{\delta} 
\beq 
we have that
\bea  
\sP\left( c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2 \right) &\,=\, \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta}  \right) \,+\, \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta}^{c}\right) \\
&\,\leq\, \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta} \right) \,+\, \frac{3}{n}
\\
&\,\leq\, \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta} \right) \,+\, \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \\
% & = \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2|\Omega_\beta} \sP\paren{\Omega_\beta} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\\ 
% & \leq \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2|\Omega_\beta}  + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}, 
\bea 
for any $\varepsilon > 0$ as $n$ large enough.
\end{proof}
By previous lemma, in consequence, it is enough to bound the term
\beq 
\label{bound_first_term}
\sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta} \right).
\beq 
% To bound $
% \sP\left(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\} \cap \Omega_{\beta} \right)$, it suffices to control  $\sP(\left\{ c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2\right\}\mid \Omega_\beta)$.  

To this end, we will work with the indicator of the coupling event.
We write $\indic{\Omega_\beta}$ for the indicator of $\Omega_\beta$.

We write $\sP(\cdot \cap \Omega_\beta)$ for the probability restricted to $\Omega_\beta$. We denote by $\Ept{x, \delta, e}{\cdot}$ the expectation taken over  $(x_{ij}, \delta_i, e_{ij})$. 
And we denote by $\Ept{e \mid x_{ij}}{\cdot}$ the conditional expectation taken over $ \left\{x_{ij} \right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_i}$ respectively.

In particular, for any nonnegative random variable $X$, we write
\[
\Ept{x, \delta, e, x^*, \delta^*, e^*}{X\,\indic{\Omega_\beta}}
\]
to denote the expectation of $X$ restricted to the coupling event $\Omega_\beta$. 
\begin{remark}
\label{remark:joint}


Moreover, on $\Omega_\beta$ we work with the coupled sample
$\{(x_{ij}^*,y_{ij}^*)\}_{i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l},j\in[m_i]}$, which are i.i.d.\ within each fixed block $\mathcal{J}_{e,l}$.
We now define the star versions of the loss quantities.
The star empirical loss is
\beq 
\label{def_hat_z_star}
\hat Z_{ij}^*(f) \,\coloneqq\, \rho_{\tau}(f^*(x_{ij}^*) + \delta_i^*(x_{ij}^*) + e_{ij}^*  - f(x_{ij}^*)) - \rho_{\tau}(\delta_i^*(x_{ij}^*) + e_{ij}^*),
\beq 
and the star conditional expectations are
\bea 
Z_{ij}^*(f) &\,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{\delta^*, e^* \mid x_{ij}^*}{ \hat Z_{ij}^*(f)}, \\
\tilde Z_{ij}^*(f) &\,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{e^* \mid \delta_i^*, x_{ij}^*}{ \hat Z_{ij}^*(f)}, \\
\bar Z_{ij}^*(f) &\,\coloneqq\,  \Ept{x^*, \delta^*, e^*}{ \hat Z_{ij}^*(f)}.
\bea 
By construction, for each fixed $l \in [L]$, the star quantities $\{\hat Z_{ij}^*(f)\}_{i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l}, j \in [m_i]}$ are built from the i.i.d.\ copies $(x_{ij}^*, e_{ij}^*, \delta_i^*)_{i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l}}$, and hence the indices $i \in \mathcal{J}_{e,l}$ are independent (similarly for $\mathcal{J}_{o,l}$). However, the copies across different blocks $l$ are not mutually independent.
On the coupling event $\Omega_\beta$ we have $(x_{ij}, e_{ij}, \delta_i) \,=\, (x_{ij}^*, e_{ij}^*, \delta_i^*)$ for all $i \in \gI$ and $j \in [m_i]$, so that
\beq 
\label{eq:Z_equal_on_Omega}
\hat Z_{ij}(f) \,=\, \hat Z_{ij}^*(f), \quad Z_{ij}(f) \,=\, Z_{ij}^*(f), \quad \tilde Z_{ij}(f) \,=\, \tilde Z_{ij}^*(f), \quad \bar Z_{ij}(f) \,=\, \bar Z_{ij}^*(f), \qquad \text{on } \Omega_\beta.
\beq 
 \end{remark}
Results that do not invoke the coupling argument remain valid under the
original probability measure and will be indicated explicitly when used.

 
% \begin{remark}
% \label{remark:joint}
% The lemmas below are stated and proved under the joint probability space with the event $\Omega_\beta$ defined in~\eqref{event_beta}.
% The probability of $\Omega_\beta^c$ has already been controlled in Lemma~\ref{lemma_beta_decomp}.
% \end{remark}

\subsubsection{Proof of Step 3}
\implementedchange{Every local process is indexed by the centered class
\[
\mathcal H_t=\{f-f^*:f\in\mathcal F,\ c_0\Delta^2(f-f^*)\le t^2\}.
\]
This class contains zero, so the relevant suprema are nonnegative and contraction is applied to maps that vanish at the origin.}
Lemma~\ref{lemma_basic_inequality} -- Corollary~\ref{extension of lemma13} are some fundamental lemmas support our convergence analysis. 

\begin{lemma}[Basic Inequality]
\label{lemma_basic_inequality}
By Definitions in \eqref{main_est} and \eqref{295}, we have 
\bea 
\hat Z(\hat f) - \hat Z(f ^*) \,\le\, 0
\bea as the basic inequality. 
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Lipschitz condition of $\hat Z_{ij}(f)$, $Z_{ij}(f)$ and $r_{ij}(f)$]
\label{lem:lip}
The quantities $\hat Z_{ij}(f)$ (defined in \eqref{348}), $Z_{ij}(f)$ (defined in \eqref{eq215}), and $r_{ij}(f)$ (defined in \eqref{def_rij}) are $1$-Lipschitz in the following sense: for any $f, g \in \mathcal{F}$ and all $(i,j)$,
\bea
\abs{\hat Z_{ij}(f) - \hat Z_{ij}(g)} &\,\leq\, \abs{f(x_{ij}) - g(x_{ij})}, \\
\abs{Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(g)} &\,\leq\, \abs{f(x_{ij}) - g(x_{ij})}, \\
\abs{r_{ij}(f) - r_{ij}(g)} &\,\leq\, \abs{f(x_{ij}) - g(x_{ij})}.
\bea
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}

For $\hat Z_{ij}(f)$ and $Z_{ij}(f)$, their Lipschitz condition can be seen in the Lemma A.11 of \cite{zhang2023risk}. 
For 
\bea r_{ij}(f) \,=\, \int_{0}^{f(x_{ij}) - f^{*}(x_{ij})} \left\{ \mathbb{P}( y_{i,j} \,\leq\, f^*(x_{ij}) + t\mid x_{ij}) - \mathbb{P}( y_{i,j} \,\leq\, f^*(x_{ij}) + t |\delta_i, x_{ij})\right\} dt.\bea
We have 
\bea 
&\abs{r_{ij}(f) - r_{ij}(g)} \\ &\,=\,
\int_{0}^{f(x_{ij}) - g(x_{ij})} \left\{ \mathbb{P}( y_{i,j} \,\leq\, f^*(x_{ij}) + t\mid x_{ij}) - \mathbb{P}( y_{i,j} \,\leq\, f^*(x_{ij}) + t |\delta_i, x_{ij})\right\} dt.\\
&\,\leq\, \abs{f(x_{ij}) - g(x_{ij})}. 
\bea 

\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma12}
For any $f, f^* \in \mathcal{F}$, let $h = f - f^*$, and let $h(x_{ij}) \,=\, (f - f^*)(x_{ij})$. Then we have 
    \bea 
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) &\,=\, \int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}))}dz.
\bea 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
        Let $I$ be the indicator function. By equation (B.3) in \cite{belloni2011},we have 
\beq
\rho_{\tau}(w-v) - \rho_{\tau}(w) \,=\, -v(\tau - I(w\,\le\, 0)) + \int_{0}^v \set{I(w \le  z) - I(w \le  0)} dz. 
\beq 

Given any $f$ and $x_{ij}$, let $w = y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij})$, $v = f(x_{ij}) - f^*(x_{ij})$. 
Then taking conditional expectations on
above equation with respect to $y_{ij}$ conditioned on $x_{ij}$ on both sides, we have 
\beq 
Z_{ij}( f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) &\,=\, \Ept{\delta, e \mid x_{ij}}{  \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f(x_{ij})) - \rho_{\tau}(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))} \\
&\,=\, \Ept{\delta, e \mid x_{ij}}{-h(x_{ij})(\tau-I(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}) \,\le\, 0))} \\
&+ \Ept{\delta, e \mid x_{ij}}{\int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{I(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}) \le  z) - I(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}))\le  0}dz}\\
&\,=\, 0 + \int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}( f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}( f^*(x_{ij}))}dz \\
&\,=\, \int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}( f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}( f^*(x_{ij}))}dz,  \\
\beq for all $i=1, \dots, n$. 

% \textcolor{red}{try to generate:}
% \bea 
% \ept{-h(x_{ij})(\tau-I(y_{ij}  - f^*(x_{ij}) \le 0))|x_{ij}}  = 0
% \bea

\end{proof}











% Given the quantile regression objective function, we wish to minimize:

% \bea \text{Objective} = \sum_{i} \rho_{\tau}(r_i) \bea

% where $ r_i $ are the residuals for each data point.

% Given the check function, the asymmetric penalty for each residual is:

% \bea \rho_{\tau}(r_i) = 
% \begin{cases} 
% \tau r_i & \text{if } r_i \geq 0 \\
% (1-\tau) r_i & \text{if } r_i < 0 
% \end{cases} \bea

% To minimize this sum, let's split the residuals into two categories:

% - Positive residuals: $ r_i^+ $
% - Negative residuals: $ r_i^- $

% Now, split the objective:

% \bea \text{Objective} = \sum_{r_i \geq 0} \tau r_i^+ + \sum_{r_i < 0} (1-\tau) r_i^- \bea

% Our goal is to find the parameter values that minimize this objective function. For this discussion, we'll consider the simplified case where we're estimating a single parameter, say a median.

% To minimize the objective function with respect to this parameter, we'd differentiate and set the result to zero:

% \bea \frac{d}{d\theta} \text{Objective} = \tau \sum_{r_i \geq 0} \frac{d r_i^+}{d\theta} + (1-\tau) \sum_{r_i < 0} \frac{d r_i^-}{d\theta} = 0 \bea

% For simplicity, if we consider $ \frac{d r_i^+}{d\theta} $ and $ \frac{d r_i^-}{d\theta} $ to be -1 (indicating that as the parameter increases, the residual decreases), then:

% \bea \tau \sum_{r_i \geq 0} -1 + (1-\tau) \sum_{r_i < 0} -1 = 0 \bea

% \bea \tau n^+ - (1-\tau) n^- = 0 \bea

% Where $ n^+ $ and $ n^- $ are the number of positive and negative residuals, respectively.

% From this, it's clear that for the objective function to be minimized, the weighted count of positive residuals (weighted by $ \tau $) must be balanced by the weighted count of negative residuals (weighted by $ 1-\tau $). This results in an equalized penalty from overestimations and underestimations, thereby validating our earlier discussion.


\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma13}
Under Assumption~\ref{assumptionA}, with $R$ and $\ubar{f}$ from Assumption~\ref{assumptionA}, let
\beq
\label{def_C0}
C_0 \,\coloneqq\, \min\left\{\frac{\ubar{f}}{2},\; \frac{R\ubar{f}}{4}\right\}.
\beq
Then for all $f, f^* \in \mathcal{F}$, we have
\bea
Z(f) \,\ge\, C_0 \Delta_{nm}^2(f - f^*).
\bea
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $h(x_{ij}) \,\coloneqq\, f(x_{ij}) - f^*(x_{ij})$. By Lemma~\ref{lemma12}, we have
\bea
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) \,=\, \int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}))}dz.
\bea

First, suppose $h(x_{ij}) \in [0, R]$. By Assumption~\ref{assumptionA}, for any $z$ with $\abs{z} \,\le\, R$, we have $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij})) \,\ge\, \ubar{f}\, z$. Therefore,
\bea
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) \,\geq\, \int_{0}^{h(x_{ij})} \ubar{f}\, z \, dz \,=\, \frac{\ubar{f}\, h(x_{ij})^2}{2}.
\bea

Next, suppose $h(x_{ij}) \in [-R, 0]$. For $z \in [h(x_{ij}), 0]$ we have $\abs{z} \,\le\, R$, so by Assumption~\ref{assumptionA}, $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij})) \,\le\, \ubar{f}\, z$ (noting $z \,\le\, 0$). Therefore,
\bea
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) &\,=\, \int_{h(x_{ij})}^{0} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij})) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z)}dz \\
& \,\geq\, \int_{h(x_{ij})}^{0} (-\ubar{f}\, z) \, dz \,=\, \frac{\ubar{f}\, h(x_{ij})^2}{2}.
\bea

Now suppose $h(x_{ij}) > R$. Since $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}$ is monotone non-decreasing, for $z \,\ge\, R/2$ we have $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) \,\ge\, F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + R/2)$. By Assumption~\ref{assumptionA} (applied with $R/2 \,\le\, R$), $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + R/2) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij})) \,\ge\, \ubar{f}\, R/2$. Therefore,
\beq
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) &\,\ge\, \int_{R/2}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + z) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}))}dz\\
&\,\ge\, \int_{R/2}^{h(x_{ij})} \set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + R/2) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}))}dz\\
&\,=\, (h(x_{ij}) - R/2)\set{F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}) + R/2) - F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}(f^*(x_{ij}))}\\
&\,\ge\, \frac{h(x_{ij})}{2}\cdot\frac{R\, \ubar{f}}{2} \,=\, \frac{R\,\ubar{f}}{4} \abs{h(x_{ij})} \,\ge\, C_0 \abs{h(x_{ij})},
\beq
where the first inequality holds since the integrand is non-negative on $[0, R/2]$, the second by monotonicity of $F_{y_{ij}|x_{ij}}$, and the penultimate uses $h(x_{ij}) - R/2 \,\ge\, h(x_{ij})/2$ (since $h(x_{ij}) > R$).

The case $h(x_{ij}) < -R$ is analogous (by reversing the direction of integration) and yields the same bound $Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) \,\ge\, C_0 \abs{h(x_{ij})}$.

Combining the four cases, for all $(i,j)$ we have
\bea
Z_{ij}(f) - Z_{ij}(f^*) \,\ge\, C_0 \min\set{\abs{h(x_{ij})}, h(x_{ij})^2}.
\bea
Summing over all $(i,j)$ and using $Z_{ij}(f^*) \,=\, 0$, we obtain
\bea
Z(f) \,\ge\, C_0 \Delta_{nm}^2(f - f^*),
\bea
where $\Delta_{nm}^2(\cdot)$ is the empirical Huber loss defined in \eqref{huber loss}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{extension of lemma13} Assume Assumption~\ref{assumptionA} holds. Then for $C_0$ defined in \eqref{def_C0} and for all $f, f^* \in \mathcal{F}$, we have
\bea
\bar Z(f) \,\ge\, C_0 \Delta^2(f-f^*),
\bea
where $\bar Z(f) \,\coloneqq\, \Ept{x, \delta, e}{\hat Z(f)}$ is defined in \eqref{305} and $\Delta^2(\cdot)$ is the population Huber loss defined in \eqref{huber_def}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lemma13}, for each realization of $\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_i}$ we have $Z(f) \,\ge\, C_0 \Delta_{nm}^2(f - f^*)$.
Taking expectation over $\{x_{ij}\}$ on both sides and using the definitions
$\bar Z(f) \,=\, \Ept{x}{ Z(f)}$ \textcolor{red}{the notation here does not match the statement}  from \eqref{305} and
$\Delta^2(f-f^*) \,=\, \Ept{x}{\Delta_{nm}^2(f-f^*)}$ from \eqref{huber_def}, we obtain the result.
\end{proof}

% \begin{remark}
% \label{remark:Omega_beta}
% In the subsequent analysis, all probabilities and expectations are understood to be conditioned on the event $\Omega_\beta$, unless otherwise specified. The probability of the complement event $\Omega_\beta^c$ has been controlled in Lemma~\ref{lemma_beta_decomp}, and thus does not affect the main results. 
% % To make more clear, we will use \cref{lemma:huber loss} as an example. 
% \end{remark}

Lemma~\ref{lemma:huber loss}--\ref{col_A_3} are used to bound the term \eqref{bound_first_term}. 


\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma:huber loss}
Let $\hat f, f^* \in \mathcal{F}$ and let $\Omega_\beta$ be as defined in \eqref{event_beta}.
Let $C_0$ be the constant from Corollary~\ref{extension of lemma13} (defined in \eqref{def_C0}), satisfying $\bar Z(f) \,\ge\, C_0 \Delta^2(f - f^*)$. Let $c_0$ be any constant satisfying
\beq
\label{eq_c0_range}
0<c_0<C_0.
\beq
Then for any $t>0$,
\bea
\sP\!\left(\{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f-f^*)>t^2\}\cap \Omega_\beta\right)
\le
\sP\!\left(\left\{\sup_{\substack{f\in\mathcal F\\ c_0\Delta^2(f-f^*)\le t^2}}\big(\bar Z(f)-\hat Z(f)-\ell(f)\big)>t^2\right\}\cap \Omega_\beta\right),
\bea
where $\ell(f)\,\coloneqq\, (C_0-c_0)\Delta^2(f-f^*)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
% By the total probability formula, we have 
% \bea
% &\sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2} =  \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2|\Omega_\beta} \sP\paren{\Omega_\beta} + \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2|\Omega_\beta^c} \sP\paren{\Omega_\beta^c} \\
% &\leq \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2|\Omega_\beta} + \sP\paren{\Omega_\beta^c}.   
% \bea 
    Assume $c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2 $, where $c_0$ satisfies \eqref{eq_c0_range}. We know $\Delta^2$ is continuous.  
    Next, define $g: \bracket{0,1} \rightarrow \R$ as $g(\mu) \,=\, c_0\Delta^2((1-\mu)f^* + \mu\hat f - f^*)$. Clearly, $g$ is a continuous function with $g(0) \,=\, 0$ and $g(1) \,=\, c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*)$.  Therefore, there exists $\mu_{\hat f} \in \bracket{0,1}$ such that $g(\mu_{\hat f}) \,=\, t^2$. We then let $\tilde f \,=\, (1-\mu_{\hat f}) f^* + \mu_{\hat f}\hat f$, so that by construction $c_0\Delta^{2}(\tilde f-f^*) \,=\, t^2$.

 Then we have 
\beq 
\label{eq436}
 \hat Z (\tilde f) \,=\, \hat Z\set{(1-\mu_{\hat f}) f^* + \mu_{\hat f}\hat f} \labelrel\le{eq392} (1-\mu_{\hat f})\hat Z(f^*) + \mu_{\hat f} \hat Z(\hat f) \labelrel\le{in3922} 0.
\beq 
Where \eqref{eq392} follows the convexity of $\hat Z$. The \eqref{in3922} follows by the $\hat Z(f^*) \,=\, 0$, and the basic inequality. 
First, let
\beq 
\label{def_df}
\ell(\tilde f)\,\coloneqq\, (C_0 - c_0)\Delta^2(\tilde f - f^*),
\beq 
Since $\hat f, f^* \in \gF$, and $\gF$ is a convex set, thus, $\tilde f \in \gF$, and $c_0\Delta^{2}(\tilde f-f^*) \,=\, t^2$,  then we have 
 \bea
   t^2 \,=\,&c_0\Delta^2(\tilde f - f^*) \\
   \,=\,&C_0\Delta^2(\tilde f - f^*) - \underbrace{(C_0 - c_0)\Delta^2(\tilde f - f^*)}_{\,\coloneqq\, \ell(\tilde f)}
   \\
    \,\le\,&  \bar Z(\tilde f) - \ell(\tilde f) \\
    \,=\,&  \bar Z(\tilde f)  - \hat Z(\tilde f) + \hat Z(\tilde f) -\ell(\tilde f)\\
     \labelrel\le{in377} &  \bar Z(\tilde f) - \hat Z(\tilde f) - \ell(\tilde f) \\
   \,\le\, &  \sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{\bar Z(f) - \hat Z(f) -\ell(f)}. 
    \bea 
    
The first inequality holds by applying the Corollary~\ref{extension of lemma13}.
The \eqref{in377} follows by expression in the line~\eqref{eq436}.

Thus, we have 
\bea
\sP\paren{\set{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2} \cap \Omega_\beta}
\le & \sP\paren{\set{\sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{\bar Z(f) - \hat Z(f) -\ell(f)} > t^2} \cap \Omega_\beta}.
\bea 
% \bea
% \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2} \le & \sP\paren{ \sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{Z(f) - \hat Z(f)} + \sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{\bar Z(f) - Z(f)} > t^2} \\
% \le & \sP\paren{  \sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{Z(f) - \hat Z(f)} > t^2/2} + \sP\paren{\sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{\bar Z(f) - Z(f)} > t^2/2}.
% \bea 
\end{proof}

% \sP\paren{c_0\Delta^2(\hat f - f^*) > t^2} \le \sP\paren{  \sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{Z(f) - \hat Z(f)} > t^2/2} + \sP\paren{\sup_{\substack{f\in \mathcal{F} \\ c_0\Delta^2(f - f^*)\le t^2}}\paren{\bar Z(f) - Z(f)} > t^2/2}.
